On Doctrines and Cartesian Bicategories
Filippo Bonchi, Alessio Santamaria, Jens Seeber, Paweł Sobociński

Abstract
We study the relationship between cartesian bicategories and a specialisation of Lawvere’s hyperdoctrines, namely elementary existential doctrines. Both provide different ways of abstracting the structural properties of logical systems: the former in algebraic terms based on a string diagrammatic calculus, the latter in universal terms using the fundamental notion of adjoint functor. We prove that these two approaches are related by an adjunction, which can be strengthened to an equivalence by imposing further constraints on doctrines.

We thank Pino Rosolini and Fabio Pasquali for discussions that improved our understanding of doctrines.

1
Introduction

In
[
20
,
21
,
22
]
Lawvere introduced the notion of
hyperdoctrine
in an effort to capture the
universal
content of logical theories, and first-order logic in particular. Here, by universal, we intend by means of
universal properties
in category theory.
The starting point is the notion of Lawvere theory
[
19
]
, the universal way of capturing the notion of algebraic theory – where the universal property is that of cartesian categories, namely categories with finite products. In terms of logical content, Lawvere theories provide the notion of
term
. Now, a hyperdoctrine is a certain contravariant functor
P
from the Lawvere theory of terms to a posetal 2-category, e.g. lattices or Heyting algebras. The basic, high-level idea is that the functor takes us from terms to
formulas
; more precisely, the objects of the Lawvere theories, which can be thought of as variable contexts, are taken to the Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra of formulas over these contexts. In this way, the concept of
quantifier
can be captured by means of a universal property – the existence of left-adjoints (existential quantification) and right-adjoints (universal quantification) to the image along
P
of the projections.

In recent years, there has been a large number
[
1
,
2
,
4
,
9
,
11
,
12
,
16
,
27
,
29
]
of contributions that use string diagrams in order to model computational phenomena of different kinds. Typically, the languages come with an equational theory, which can be used to reason about systems via diagrammatic reasoning. Interestingly, the
same
algebraic structures seem to appear in many different contexts, e.g. commutative monoids and comonoids, Frobenius algebras, Hopf algebras, etc. These applications, while using the language and tools of (monoidal) category theory, are of rather different nature than the more established “universal” approaches, such as Lawvere theories and hyperdoctrines sketched in the previous paragraph. A bridge between the universal and algebraic worlds is given by a theorem of Fox
[
14
]
that characterises cartesian categories as symmetric monoidal categories where each object is equipped with a well-behaved commutative comonoid structure. This means that any Lawvere theory can be seen concretely as a string diagrammatic language (see e.g.,
[
5
]
). More recently, the notion of discrete cartesian restriction category was characterised in a similar way
[
10
]
, with partial Frobenius algebras taking the place of commutative comonoids. This raises a natural question: can we capture the universal content of logical theories algebraically in a similar way? In other words, what are the “Fox theorems” for logic?

In this paper we turn our attention to the regular fragment of first-order logic with equality: formulas are built up from terms and the equality relation using the existential quantifier and conjunction. There has been much work on categorifying this fragment, notably the significant corpus of work on allegories
[
15
]
. More relevant to our story, we focus on the contrast between universal and algebraic approaches. A universal treatment, the notion of
elementary existential doctrine
, was introduced in
[
25
]
. The basic setup is the same as for Lawvere’s hyperdoctrines, but one asks only for the left adjoints, which, as we have previously mentioned, are the universal explanation for existential quantifiers. On the algebraic side, the concept that stands out is that of Carboni and Walters’ cartesian bicategories (of relations)
[
8
]
, which are symmetric monoidal categories where objects are equipped with a special Frobenius algebra and a lax-natural commutative comonoid structures. While Carboni and Walters emphasised the relational algebraic aspects, they were certainly aware of the logical connections. In fact, some recent works
[
3
,
13
,
28
]
exploited various ramifications of the correspondence between cartesian bicategories and regular logic.

Our goal for this paper is a “Fox theorem” for the regular fragment, connecting the universal and the algebraic approaches. Our starting observation is that, given a cartesian bicategory
(\mathbb{B},\otimes,I)
, one obtains an elementary existential doctrine by restricting the hom functor
\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(-,I)\colon\mathbb{B}^{\opposite}\to\Set
to the (cartesian) category of
maps
of
\mathbb{B}
.
In Remark 2.5 of
[
26
]
, it is mentioned that the other direction is also possible: given an elementary existential doctrine one can construct a cartesian bicategory. We explore the ramifications of this remark in detail. We show that these two translations are functorial and, actually, that they form an adjunction. More precisely, it turns out that the category of cartesian bicategories is a reflective subcategory of the category of doctrines.

The adjunction, however, is
not
an equivalence. We prove this with a counterexample that captures the crux of the matter:
there are doctrines
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
where the indexing categories of terms
\mathbb{C}
are not tailored to the represented logics. In doctrines-as-logical-theories, roughly speaking, equality can come from two places: implicitly, from the indexing term category, and explicitly, via logical equivalence. Doctrines, therefore, have an additional degree of intensionality: doctrines that “substantially represent” the same logic may have distinct index categories and thus not be isomorphic. This issue does not arise in cartesian bicategories where the role of
\mathbb{C}
is played by the subcategory of maps: maps are arrows satisfying certain properties, rather than given a priori in a fixed index category.

We conclude by observing that, by adding further constraints to the notion of elementary existential doctrine, namely comprehensive diagonals and the Rule of Unique Choice from
[
26
]
, it is possible to exclude such problematic doctrines. By doing so, we restrict the adjunction to an equivalence, thus obtaining a satisfactory “Fox theorem”.

Notation
.

Given
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to Z
morphisms in some category, we denote their composite as
f\mathrel{;}g
,
g\circ f
or
gf
. We write
P_{f}
for the action of a doctrine
P
on a morphism
f
.

2
Cartesian Categories

The staring point of our exposition is the definition of cartesian category that, thanks to the results of Fox
[
14
]
,
can be given in the following form, which is particularly convenient for the purposes of this paper.

.

A cartesian category is a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{C},\otimes,I)

};(f\otimes g)
for all arrows
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon X\to Z
.

Hereafter, we will use
\times
to denote both the cartesian product of sets and the categorical product in an arbitrary cartesian category. It is worth to remark that while
\Set
(sets and functions) and
\Rel
(sets and relations) are both cartesian categories, the categorical product in
\Set
is indeed the cartesian product, while in
\Rel
it is actually the disjoint union.

Example 2
.

Another cartesian category that will play an important role is
\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma}
, the
Lawvere theory

[
19
]
generated by a cartesian signature
\Sigma
(a set of symbols
f
equipped with some arity
\mathit{ar}(f)\in\mathbb{N}
). In
\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma}
, objects are natural numbers and arrows are tuples of terms over a countable set of variables
V=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots\}
. More precisely, arrows from
n
to
m
are tuples
\langle t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\rangle
of sort
(n,m)
as defined by the inference rules in the first line of Figure
1
. It is easy to check that
\langle t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\rangle
has sort
(n,m)
if each term
t_{i}
has variables in
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\}
. Composition is defined by (simultaneous) substitution: the composition of
\langle t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\rangle\colon(n,m)
with
\langle s_{1},\dots,s_{l}\rangle\colon(m,l)
is the tuple
\langle u_{1},\dots,u_{l}\rangle\colon(n,l)
where
u_{i}=s_{i}[^{t_{1}\dots t_{m}}/_{x_{1}\dots x_{m}}]
for all
i=1,\dots,l
.
One can readily check that
\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma}
is a SMC having
(\mathbb{N},+,0)
as the monoid of objects, i.e., it is a
prop
(product and permutation category, see
[
18
,
23
]
). Identities
id_{n}
and symmetries
\sigma_{n,m}
are defined as expected;

Definition 3
.

A morphism of cartesian categories is a strict monoidal functor preserving the chosen comonoid structures.

Example 4
.

Let
\Sigma_{1}
be the cartesian signature consisting of a single symbol
f
with arity
1
, and
\Sigma_{2}
be the signature with two symbols,
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
, both of arity
1
.
Consider the corresponding Lawvere theories
\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{1}}
and
\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{2}}
.
The assignment
f\mapsto g_{1}
induces a morphism of cartesian categories, hereafter denoted by
F_{1}\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{1}}\to\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{2}}
. Similarly, let
F_{2}\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{1}}\to\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{2}}
denote the morphism of cartesian categories where
f
is mapped to
g_{2}
.
Finally, there is a unique morphism of cartesian categories
Q\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{2}}\to\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{1}}
mapping
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
to
f
.

\begin{gathered}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 7.52196pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{i\leq n}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=37.82462pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${(V)}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle x_{i}\colon(n,1)$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{f\in\Sigma\quad\mathit{ar}(f)=m\quad\langle t_{1},\dots t_{m}\rangle\colon(n,m)}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=162.97243pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${(\Sigma)}$}}}\hbox{\kern 42.3745pt\hbox{$\displaystyle f\langle t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\rangle\colon(n,1)$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{t_{1}\colon(n,1)\quad\langle t_{2},\dots,t_{m}\rangle\colon(n,m-1)}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=135.68631pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${\langle\dots\rangle}$}}}\hbox{\kern 29.82751pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\langle t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\rangle\colon(n,m)$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 18.27893pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=36.55786pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${\langle\rangle}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\langle\rangle\colon(n,0)$}}}}\\[15.00002pt]
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 19.39001pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=38.78003pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${(\top)}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\top\colon(n,0)$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{P\in\mathbb{P}\quad\mathit{ar}(P)=m\quad\langle t_{1},\dots t_{m}\rangle\colon(n,m)}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=162.67383pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${(\mathbb{P})}$}}}\hbox{\kern 41.30681pt\hbox{$\displaystyle P\langle t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\rangle\colon(n,0)$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 3.69736pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\quad\phi\colon(n+1,0)$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=72.13301pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${(\exists)}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\exists x_{n+1}.\phi\colon(n,0)$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty{\langle t_{1},t_{2}\rangle\colon(n,2)}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=57.19678pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$(=)$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.83336pt\hbox{$\displaystyle t_{1}=t_{2}\colon(n,0)$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\phi\colon(n,0)\quad\psi\colon(n,0)$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=85.58784pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$(\wedge)$}}}\hbox{\kern 15.22339pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\phi\wedge\psi\colon(n,0)$}}}}\end{gathered}

Figure 1:
Sort inference rules

3
Elementary Existential Doctrines

Recall that an
inf-semilattice
is a partially ordered set with all finite infima, including a top element
\top
. We denote the category of inf-semilattices and inf-preserving functions by
\InfSL
.

The following definition is taken almost verbatim from
[
26
]
. The difference is that there the base category
\mathbb{C}
only needs binary products, whereas we also require a terminal object.

Definition 5
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a cartesian category.
An elementary existential doctrine is given by a functor
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL

that is:

•

Elementary
, namely for every object
A
in
\mathbb{C}
there is an element
\delta_{A}\in P(A\times A)
such that
for every map
e=id_{X}\times\Delta_{A}\colon X\times A\to X\times A\times A
,
the function
P_{e}\colon P(X\times A\times A)\to P(X\times A)
has a left adjoint
\exists_{e}\colon P(X\times A)\to P(X\times A\times A)
defined by the assignment

\exists_{e}(\alpha)=P_{\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle\colon X\times A\times A\to X\times A}(\alpha)\wedge P_{\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{3}\rangle\colon X\times A\times A\to A\times A}(\delta_{A}).

(1)

•

Existential
, namely for every
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
and projection
\pi_{i}\colon A_{1}\times A_{2}\to A_{i}
with
i\in\{1,2\}
,
the function
P_{\pi_{i}}\colon P(A_{i})\to P(A_{1}\times A_{2})
has a left-adjoint
\exists_{\pi_{i}}
that satisfies

–

the
Beck-Chevalley condition
:
for any projection
\pi\colon X\times A\to A
and any pullback

\hbox to101.83pt{\vbox to49.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 50.91502pt\lower-24.11165pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-50.91502pt}{-19.6186pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

(2)

–

Frobenius reciprocity
:
for any projection
\pi\colon X\times A\to A
,
\alpha\in P(A)
and
\beta\in P(X)
, it holds that

\exists_{\pi}(P_{\pi}(\alpha)\wedge\beta)=\alpha\wedge\exists_{\pi}(\beta)
.

Remark 6
.

Taking
X
in (
1
) to be the terminal object of
\mathbb{C}
, one obtains that the function
\exists_{\Delta_{A}}\colon P(A)\to P(A\times A)
given by

\exists_{\Delta_{A}}(\alpha)=P_{\pi_{1}}(\alpha)\wedge\delta_{A}

(3)

is left-adjoint to
P_{\Delta_{A}}\colon P(A\times A)\to P(A)
. This condition, which appears in
[
26
]
, is therefore redundant when
\mathbb{C}
has terminal object.

Remark 7
.

In any cartesian category, the diagram below is a pullback of
f\colon A^{\prime}\to A
with a projection
\pi\colon X\times A\to A
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{X}\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Therefore, given a functor
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
, to check that
P
satisfies the Beck-Chevalley condition it suffices to show that (
2
) holds for
X^{\prime}\!=X\times A^{\prime}
,
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{2}
and
f^{\prime}=\id_{X}\times f
.

The contravariant powerset
\pazocal P
, while often seen as an endofunctor on
\Set
, can also be seen as a functor

\pazocal P\colon\Set^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
. It is the classical example of an elementary existential doctrine. Recall that, for
f\colon Y\to X
in
\Set
,
\pazocal P_{f}\colon\pazocal P(X)\to\pazocal P(Y)
is
\pazocal P_{f}(Z)=\{y\in Y\mid f(y)\in Z\}
. Given a projection
\pi\colon X\times A\to A
,
\pazocal P_{\pi}
and its left adjoint
\exists_{\pi}
are as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal P(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal P(X\times A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{(x,b)\in X\times A\mid b\in B\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal P_{\pi}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal P(X\times A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal P(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{a\in A\mid\exists x\in X\ldotp(x,a)\in S\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists_{\pi}}

For every set
A
,
\delta_{A}
is fixed to be
\{(a,a)\mid a\in A\}\in\pazocal P(A\times A)
. With this information, it is easy to check that
\pazocal P\colon\Set^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
satisfies the conditions in Definition
5
. For the convenience of the reader, we work out the details in Appendix
A
.

Example 8
.

Let
\Sigma
and
\mathbb{P}
be signatures of function and predicate symbols respectively. The regular fragment of first order logic consists of formulas built from conjunction
\wedge
, true
\top
, existential quantification
\exists
, equality
t_{1}=t_{2}
and atoms
P\langle t_{1},\dots t_{m}\rangle
where
P\in\mathbb{P}
and
t_{i}
are terms over
\Sigma
. Formulas are sorted according to the rules in Figure
1
:
\phi
has sort
(n,0)
if the free variables of
\phi
are in
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
.

The indexed Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra functor
LT\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
assigns to each
n\in\mathbb{N}
the set of formulas of sort
(n,0)
modulo logical equivalence (defined in the usual way). These form a semilattice with top given by
\top
and meet by
\wedge
, where
\phi\leq\psi
if and only if
\psi
is a logical consequence of
\phi
.
To the arrow
\langle t_{1},\dots t_{m}\rangle\colon n\to m
,
LT
assigns the substitution mapping each
\phi\colon(m,0)
to
\phi[^{t_{1},\dots t_{m}}/_{x_{1},\dots x_{m}}]\colon(n,0)
. In particular, for the projection
\pi\colon n+m\to n
(that is
\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\rangle\colon n+m\to n
)
LT_{\pi}
maps a formula of sort
(n,0)
to the same formula but with sort
(n+m,0)
1
1

1

The second projection
\pi\colon n+m\to m
requires the reindexing of variables. We do not discuss this case in order to keep the presentation simpler.
. Its left adjoint
\exists_{\pi}
maps a formula
\phi\colon(n+m,0)
to the formula
\exists x_{n+1}.\dots\exists x_{n+m}.\,\phi\colon(n,0)
. The Beck-Chevalley condition asserts that “substitution commutes with quantification”: if
\phi
is a formula with at most
x_{n+1}
free variables and
t
is a term that does not contain
x_{n+1}
, then
\exists x_{n+1}.\,(\phi[^{t}/_{x_{i}}])=(\exists x_{n+1}.\,\phi)[^{t}/_{x_{i}}]
. Frobenius reciprocity states that
\exists x_{i}.\,(\phi\wedge\psi)=\phi\wedge(\exists x_{i}.\,\psi)
if
x_{i}
is not a free variable of
\phi
. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\delta_{n}
is the formula
(x_{1}=x_{n+1})\wedge(x_{2}=x_{n+2})\wedge\dots\wedge(x_{n}=x_{n+n})
.

Definition 9

(Cf.
[
26
]
)
.

The category
\mathsf{EED}
consists of the following data.

•

Objects are elementary existential doctrines
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
.

•

Morphisms from
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
to
R\colon\mathbb{D}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
are pairs
(F,b)
, where
F\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
is a strict cartesian functor while
b\colon P\to R\circ F^{\opposite}
is a natural transformation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\InfSL}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}^{\opposite}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\opposite}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

that preserves equalities and existential quantifiers, that is
b_{A\times A}(\delta^{P}_{A})=\delta^{R}_{F(A)}
for all
A
in
\mathbb{C}
and, for any projection
\pi\colon X\times A\to A
in
\mathbb{C}
, the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RF(X\times A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RF(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists^{P}_{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{X\times A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists^{R}_{F(\pi)}}

(4)

•

Composition of
(F,b)\colon P\to R
as above with
(G,c)\colon R\to S
is given by
(GF,cF\circ b)
.

Remark 10
.

In
[
26
]
, morphisms of elementary existential doctrines
P\to R
are pairs
(F,b)
where
F\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
is a functor between the base categories that preserves binary products merely up to isomorphism, so
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(X\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\pi_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\pi_{2})}

is a product diagram of
F(X)
and
F(Y)
in
\mathbb{D}
but it might not coincide with the
chosen
product of
\mathbb{D}
. For this reason, preservation of equality for
b
in
[
26
]
means that
b_{A\times A}(\delta^{P}_{A})=R_{\langle F(\pi_{1}),F(\pi_{2})\rangle}(\delta^{R}_{F(A)})
.

Remark 11
.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{D}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be an elementary existential doctrine and
F\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{D}
a strict cartesian functor. Then
P\circ F^{\opposite}\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
is again an elementary existential doctrine, where
\delta^{PF^{\opposite}}_{A}=\delta^{P}_{F(A)}
for all
A
in
\mathbb{B}
and, for very
\pi_{i}\colon X_{1}\times X_{2}\to X_{i}
,
\exists^{PF^{\opposite}}_{\pi_{i}}=\exists^{P}_{F(\pi_{i})}
.

4
Cartesian Bicategories

In this section we recall from
[
8
]
definitions and properties of cartesian bicategories.

Definition 12
.

A cartesian bicategory is a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathbb{B},\otimes,I)
enriched over the category of posets (that is every hom-set is a partial order and both composition and
\otimes
are monotonous operations) where every object
X\in\mathbb{B}
is equipped with morphisms

In the original definition of

The archetypal example of a cartesian bicategory is the category of sets and relations
\Rel
, with cartesian product of sets as monoidal product and
1=\{\bullet\}
as unit
I
. To be precise,
\Rel
has sets as objects and relations
R\subseteq X\times Y
as arrows
X\to Y
. Composition and monoidal product are defined as expected:

R\mathrel{;}S=\{(x,z)\,|\,\exists y\text{ s.t.\ }(x,y)\in R\text{ and }(y,z)\in S\}
and

R\otimes S=\{\big((x_{1},x_{2})\,,\,(y_{1},y_{2})\big)\,|\,(x_{1},y_{1})\in R\text{ and }(x_{2},y_{2})\in S\}
.

X
, the comonoid structure is given by the diagonal function

and the unique function

}=\{(\bullet,x)\,|\,x\in X\}
.
Following the analogy with
\Rel
, we will often call ‘relations’ arbitrary morphisms of a cartesian bicategory.

One of the fundamental properties of cartesian bicategories that follows from the existence of right adjoints (Property
2
in Definition
12
) is that
every local poset
\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(X,Y)
allows to take the intersection of relations and has a top element.

Lemma 13
.

Let
\mathbb{B}
be a cartesian bicategory and
X,Y\in\mathbb{B}
. The poset
\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(X,Y)
has a top element given by

\scriptstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{Y}

and the meet of relations

R,S\colon X\to Y
is given by

R
S

The Frobenius law (Property
3
) gives a compact closed structure – in other words, it allows us
to bend wires around. The cup of this compact closed structure is

, the cap analogously

and the Frobenius law implies
the snake equations:

=
=

(5)

To obtain an intuition for the lax comonoid homomorphism condition (Property
4
), it is useful to spell out its meaning in
\Rel
: in the first inequality, the left and the right-hand side are, respectively, the relations
\{\big(x,(y,y)\big)\,|\,(x,y)\in R\}
and
\{\big(x,(y,z)\big)\,|\,(x,y)\in R\text{ and }(x,z)\in R\}
, while in the second inequality, they are the relations

\{(x,\bullet)\,|\,\exists y\in Y\text{ s.t. }(x,y)\in R\}
and
\{(x,\bullet)\,|\,x\in X\}
.
It is immediate to see that the two left-to-right inclusions hold for any relation
R\subseteq X\times Y
, while the right-to-left inclusions hold for exactly those relations that are (graphs of) functions: the inclusions specify, respectively,
single-valuedness and totality.

Definition 14
.

A map in a cartesian bicategory is an arrow
f
that is a comonoid homomorphism, i.e. for which the equalities in Definition
1
.2 hold. For
\mathbb{B}
a cartesian bicategory, its category of maps,
\Map(\mathbb{B})
, has the same objects of
\mathbb{B}
and as morphisms the maps of
\mathbb{B}
.

Lemma 15
.

A morphism

f

is a map if and only if it has a right adjoint – a morphism
R
such that

f
R
\leq

and

\leq
f
R

.

As expected, maps in
\Rel
are precisely functions. Thus,
\Map(\Rel)
is exactly
\Set
. For maps it makes sense to imagine a flow of information from left to right. We will therefore draw

f

to denote a map
f
. Note that we use lower-case letters for maps and upper-case for arbitrary morphisms.
Since

\scriptstyle{X}

and

\scriptstyle{X}

are maps by Lemma
15
and since by definition, every map respects them, then

\Map(\mathbb{B})
, which inherits the monoidal product from
\mathbb{B}
, has the structure of a cartesian category (Definition
1
).

Lemma 16
.

For a cartesian bicategory
\mathbb{B}
, the monoidal product
\otimes
is a product on
\Map(\mathbb{B})
, and the monoidal unit
I
is terminal.
In other words,
(\Map(\mathbb{B}),\,\otimes,\,I)
is a cartesian category.

Definition 17
.

A morphism of cartesian bicategories is a strict monoidal functor that preserves the ordering, the chosen monoid and the comonoid structures. Cartesian bicategories and their morphisms form a category
\mathsf{CBC}
.

Proposition 18
.

Let
F\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
be a morphism of cartesian bicategories. Then restricting its domain to
\Map(\mathbb{A})
yields a strict cartesian functor
F\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\Map(\mathbb{A})}\colon\Map(\mathbb{A})\to\Map(\mathbb{B})
.

The remaining sections focus on the relationship between cartesian bicategories and elementary existential doctrines. First, a little taste of the similarity between them.

Figure 2:
Sort inference rules (top) and axioms (bottom) for
\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}}
.

Example 19
.

In Example
8
, we outlined the Lindenbaum-Tarski doctrine for the regular fragment of first order logic. We now introduce a cartesian bicategory, denoted by
\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}}
, that provides a string diagrammatic calculus for this fragment. Like Lawvere theories,
\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}}
has
(\mathbb{N},+,0)
as monoid of objects. Arrows are (equivalence classes of) string diagrams
[
32
]
generated according to the rules in Figure
2
(top).

12

\leq_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}}
be the precongruence with respect to
;
and
\otimes
generated by the axioms and
=_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}}
the corresponding equivalence, i.e.,
\leq_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}}\cap\geq_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}}
. Now
\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}}[n,m]
is exactly the set of
=_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}}
-equivalence classes of diagrams
d\colon n\to m
ordered by
\leq_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}}
. Simple inductions suffice to check that
\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}}
is indeed a cartesian bicategory and that, moreover,
\Map(\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}})
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma}
.

In Figure
3
we introduce a function
\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket
that translates sorted formulas
\phi\colon(n,0)
into string diagrams of type
n\to 0
. From a general result in
[
31
]
, it follows that
\phi
is a logical consequence of
\psi
if and only if
\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket\leq_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}}\left\llbracket\phi\right\rrbracket
. For an example take
\psi\equiv\exists x_{2}.\,P(x_{2},x_{1})\wedge f(x_{1})=x_{2}\colon(1,0)
and
\phi\equiv\exists x_{2}.\,P(x_{2},x_{1})\colon(1,0)
. Then
\left\llbracket\psi\right\rrbracket
is the leftmost string diagram below, while
\left\llbracket\phi\right\rrbracket
is the rightmost one. The following derivation proves that
\exists x_{2}.\,P(x_{2},x_{1})
is a logical consequence of
\exists x_{2}.\,P(x_{2},x_{1})\wedge f(x_{1})=x_{2}
.

5
From Cartesian Bicategories to Doctrines

In this section we illustrate how a cartesian bicategory
\mathbb{B}
gives rise to an elementary existential doctrine.
The starting observation is that, using the conclusion of Lemma
13
, the functor
\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(-,I)\colon\mathbb{B}^{\opposite}\to\Set
sends objects
X
to Inf-semilattices. However, for an arbitrary morphism
R\colon X\to Y
,
\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(R,I)\colon\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(Y,I)\to\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(X,I)
may
not
be an inf-preserving function. A sufficient condition is to require
R
to be a map; indeed, in that case it is immediate to see that infima, as defined in Lemma
13
, are preserved.
Thus, by restricting the domain of the
\Hom
-functor to
\Map(\mathbb{B})^{\opposite}
, one obtains a contravariant functor from the cartesian category
\Map(\mathbb{B})
(Lemma
16
) to
\InfSL
:

\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})=\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(-,I)\colon\Map(\mathbb{B})^{\opposite}\to\InfSL.

(6)

Theorem 20
.

The functor
\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})
in (
6
) is an elementary existential doctrine.

Proof.

We need to show that
\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})
is elementary and existential.

1.

\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})
is elementary. We fix

To show that
\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})

be a projection. Then
\exists_{\pi}

For the Beck-Chevalley condition, consider the diagram in Remark
7
.
We need to show that

\exists_{\pi_{2}}(\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})_{\id_{X}\times f}(\beta))=\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})_{f}(\exists_{\pi}(\beta))
.
Translated to diagrams,

f
=
X
\beta
A
f

\beta

which holds trivially.

For Frobenius reciprocity, take
a projection
\pi\colon X\times A\to A
,
\alpha\in\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})(A)
and
\beta\in\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})(X\times A)
.
We need that

\exists_{\pi}(\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})_{\pi}(\alpha)\wedge\beta)=\alpha\wedge\exists_{\pi}(\beta)
, which translates to

X
\beta
A
A
\alpha
X
=
X
\beta
A
A
\alpha

This holds by naturality of the symmetry and since

is the counit of

. ∎

By applying
\pazocal{R}
to
the cartesian bicategory
\Rel
, one obtains
\pazocal P\colon\Set^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
, in the sense that
\pazocal P\cong\pazocal{R}(\Rel)
in
\mathsf{EED}
. The isomorphism is the pair
(\Gamma,b)
, where
\Gamma\colon\Set\to\Map(\Rel)
is the strict cartesian functor computing the graph of a function and
b\colon\pazocal P\to\pazocal{R}(\Rel)\circ\Gamma^{\opposite}
is the natural transformation defined for all sets
A
and
S\in\pazocal P(A)
as
b_{A}(S)=\{(s,\bullet)\mid s\in S\}
.

Example 21
.

Consider
\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,P}
of Example
19
: then
\pazocal{R}(\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,P})
is isomorphic to the doctrine
LT\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
of Example
8
. Here is why: the first two rows in Figure
3
define a cartesian isomorphism
F_{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket}\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma}\to\Map(\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}})
. For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
b_{n}\colon LT(n)\to\Hom(F_{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket}(n),0)=\Hom(n,0)
as the function mapping
\phi\colon(n,0)
to
\left\llbracket\phi\right\rrbracket\colon n\to 0
. This give rise to a natural isomorphism
b\colon LT\to\pazocal{R}(\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}})\circ F_{\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket}^{\opposite}
.

}}
. The pair
(F_{\left\llbracket-\right\rrbracket},b)
witnesses the isomorphism of
LT
and
\pazocal{R}(\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}})
.

Proposition 22
.

Assigning doctrines to cartesian bicategories as in (
6
) extends to a functor
\pazocal{R}\colon\mathsf{CBC}\to\mathsf{EED}
. For a morphism of cartesian bicategories
F\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
in
\mathsf{CBC}
,

\pazocal{R}(F)=({F}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\Map(\mathbb{A})},b^{F})

and
b^{F}\colon\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{A})\to\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})\circ(F\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\Map(\mathbb{A})})^{\opposite}
is defined as

b^{F}_{A}(U)=F(U)\in\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(F(A),I)\quad\text{for all $A\in\mathbb{A}$ and $U\in\Hom_{\mathbb{A}}(A,I)$}.

(7)

Proof.

Let
F\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
be a morphism in
\mathsf{CBC}
. By Proposition
18
, we have that
F\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\Map(\mathbb{A})}
is a strict cartesian functor.
Regarding
b^{F}
, its naturality is ensured by (in fact, equivalent to) the functoriality of
F
, while the preservation of equalities and existential quantifiers of
\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{A})
follows from the fact that
F
preserves the structure of cartesian bicategory of
\mathbb{A}
, which is used to define the structure of elementary existential doctrine of
\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{A})
. Therefore
\pazocal{R}(F)
is indeed a morphism in
\mathsf{EED}
.
Preservation of compositions and identities is straightforward.
∎

6
From Doctrines to Cartesian Bicategories

Given an elementary existential doctrine
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
, we can form a category
\mathscr{A}_{P}
whose objects are the same as
\mathbb{C}
and whose morphisms are given by the elements of
P(X\times Y)
, intuitively seen as
relations
, as observed in
[
26
]
. Inspired by the calculus of ordinary relations on sets, using the structure of
P
we can define a notion of composition and tensor product of these inner relations, and endow each object with a comonoid structure that makes
\mathscr{A}_{P}
a cartesian bicategory. Here we recall the essential definitions, while the proof of the fact that
\mathscr{A}_{P}
actually satisfies Definition
12
is rather laborious and therefore omitted here: the interested reader can find it in all details in Appendix
B
.

Since
\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(X,Y)=P(X\times Y)
, we get that
\mathscr{A}_{P}
is poset-enriched. Given that
P
is elementary, the obvious candidate for identity on
X
is

\delta_{X}\in\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(X,X)
. Composition works as follows:
let
f\in\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(X,Y)=P(X\times Y)
and
g\in\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(Y,Z)=P(Y\times Z)
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}

Consider the projections above. Then the composite
f\mathrel{;}g
is defined as

f\mathrel{;}g=\exists_{\pi_{Y}}(P_{\pi_{Z}}(f)\wedge P_{\pi_{X}}(g)).

The monoidal structure of
\mathscr{A}_{P}
is very straightforward: on objects, the monoidal product
\otimes
is given by the cartesian product in
\mathbb{C}
.
On morphisms, for
f\in\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(A,B)=P(A\times B)
and
g\in\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(C,D)=P(C\times D)
,
consider
the projections

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times C\times B\times D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\times D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{3}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{4}\rangle}

Then
let

f\otimes g=P_{\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{3}\rangle}(f)\wedge P_{\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{4}\rangle}(g).

This makes
\mathscr{A}_{P}
a monoidal, poset-enriched category. The rest of the structure of cartesian bicategory is inherited from
\mathbb{C}
by means of a crucial tool: the
graph functor
of
P
, hereafter denoted by
\Gamma_{P}\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathscr{A}_{P}
. It is the identity on objects and sends arrows
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{C}
to

\Gamma_{P}(f)=P_{f\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y})\in P(X\times Y)=\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(X,Y).

Proposition 23
.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be an elementary, existential doctrine. Then:

•

\Gamma_{P}
is strict monoidal,

•

\Gamma_{P}(f)
has a right adjoint, namely
P_{\id_{Y}\times f}(\delta_{Y})
, for every
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{C}
. Therefore, by Lemma
15
, it corestricts to a strict cartesian functor
\Gamma_{P}\colon\mathbb{C}\to\Map(\mathscr{A}_{P})
.

Consider for instance the powerset doctrine
\pazocal P\colon\Set^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
: the elements of
\pazocal P(X\times Y)
are precisely the relations from
X
to
Y
, while composition and monoidal product in
\mathscr{A}P
coincide with the usual composition and product of relations, see §
4
. In other words,
\mathscr{A}_{\pazocal P}=\Rel
. The functor
\Gamma_{\pazocal P}
calculates graphs of functions, which are exactly the maps in
\Rel
.

Example 24
.

Recall the doctrine
LT\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma}\to\InfSL
and the cartesian bicategory
\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,\mathbb{P}}
from Examples
8
and
19
. The functor
\Gamma_{LT}\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma}\to\Map(\mathscr{A}_{LT})
is inductively defined as the unique cartesian functor mapping each
f\in\Sigma
with arity
\mathit{ar}(f)=n
to the formula
f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=x_{n+1}\colon(n+1,0)
.

Now, since
\mathbb{C}
is a cartesian category, every object is canonically equipped with a natural and coherent comonoid structure. We can use the strict monoidal functor
\Gamma_{P}\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathscr{A}_{P}
to transport this comonoid to
\mathscr{A}_{P}
, and by Proposition
23
we have that copying and discarding in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
both have right adjoints.
Finally, one can prove that every morphism in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
is a lax-comonoid homomorphism and that the Frobenius law holds.

Theorem 25
.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be an elementary existential doctrine. Then
\mathscr{A}_{P}
is a cartesian bicategory. Moreover, the assignment
P\mapsto\mathscr{A}_{P}
extends to a functor
\pazocal{L}\colon\mathsf{EED}\to\mathsf{CBC}
as follows:
for
P
as above,
R\colon\mathbb{D}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
and
(F,b)\colon P\to R
in
\mathsf{EED}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}(F,b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times Y)\ni r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{X\times Y}(r)\in R(F(X)\times F(Y))}

7
An Adjunction

We saw in Sections
5
and
6
that using
\pazocal{L}
and
\pazocal{R}
one can pass, in a functorial way, between the worlds of cartesian bicategories and elementary existential doctrines. Here we show that, in fact, they define an adjunction
\pazocal{L}\dashv\pazocal{R}
. For this we need natural transformations

\eta\colon\id_{\mathsf{EED}}\to\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L},\qquad\varepsilon\colon\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}\to\id_{\mathsf{CBC}}

that make the following triangles commute for every
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
in
\mathsf{EED}
and
\mathbb{B}
in
\mathsf{CBC}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}(P)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}(\eta_{P})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\pazocal{L}(P)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{\pazocal{L}(P)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{R}(\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}})}

(8)

Let us start with
\varepsilon
. Recall that
\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})=\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(-,I)\colon\Map(\mathbb{B})^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
and that, for
f\colon X\to Y
in
\Map(\mathbb{B})
and
U\in\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(Y,I)
,

\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})_{f}(U)
is equal to
U\circ f\colon X\to I
. By definition then,
\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})=\mathscr{A}_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
has the same objects of
\mathbb{B}
while a morphism
X\to Y
in
\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})
is an element of
\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(X\times Y,I)
. Hence,
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}
has to take a

R
\scriptstyle X
\scriptstyle Y

in
\mathbb{B}
and produce a morphism
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}(R)\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{B}
. We can do so by “bending” the
Y
string, defining:

\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}\left(\scalebox{0.7}{\hbox to31.21pt{\vbox to19.88pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 21.83957pt\lower-8.94966pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}

and it is immediate to see that
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}(R)=R
and
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}(S)=S
.

Example 26
.

Recall from Example
21
that
\pazocal{R}(\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,P})\cong LT
. Applying
\pazocal{L}
to both, one gets
\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,P})\cong\pazocal{L}(LT)=\mathscr{A}_{LT}
, hence using
\varepsilon
we have that
\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,P}\cong\mathscr{A}_{LT}
. In particular, given a formula
\phi\colon(n+m,0)
, one obtains a morphism
n\to m
in
\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,P}
by first translating
\phi
to the string diagram
\left\llbracket\phi\right\rrbracket\colon n+m\to 0
(which is a morphism in
\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathsf{CB}_{\Sigma,P})
of type
n\to m
), and then bending the last
m
inputs using
\varepsilon
as illustrated in (
9
).

Remark 27
.

{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}
coincides with
\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
on
\Map(\mathbb{B})
, since
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}(f)=\delta^{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}_{Y}\circ(f\times\id_{Y})=\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}(f)
when
f\colon X\to Y
is a map. In other words,
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}
is an extension of
\Gamma_{P}
to the whole of
\mathbb{B}
.

Regarding
\eta
, we have
\pazocal{L}(P)=\mathscr{A}_{P}
, whose objects are the objects of
\mathbb{C}
, and hom-sets are
\mathscr{A}_{P}(X,Y)=P(X\times Y)
. This means that

\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)=\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(-,I)=P({-}\times I)\colon\Map({\mathscr{A}_{P}})^{\opposite}\to\InfSL.

To give a morphism
\eta_{P}\colon P\to\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)
in
\mathsf{EED}
means therefore to give a functor
F\colon\mathbb{C}\to\Map(\mathscr{A}_{P})
and a natural transformation
b\colon P\to P(F(-)\times I)
satisfying certain conditions. We have a natural candidate for
F
: we proved in Proposition
23
that
\Gamma_{P}
is a functor whose image, in fact, is included in
\Map(\mathscr{A}_{P})
and, moreover, it is cartesian. Being the identity on objects, the natural transformation part of the definition of
\eta_{P}
must have components
b_{X}\colon P(X)\to P(X\times I)
: it is clear then that the natural transformation

P\rho=\bigl(P_{\rho_{X}}\colon P(X)\to P(X\times I)\bigr)_{X\in\mathbb{C}},

obtained by whiskering
P
with the right unitor
\rho_{X}\colon X\times I\to X
of
\mathbb{C}
, is a sensible choice for
b
. In short, we define

\eta=\bigl((\Gamma_{P},P\rho)\colon P\to\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)\bigr)_{P\in\mathsf{EED}}.

(10)

The interested reader can find a proof of the fact that
\eta
and
\varepsilon
are well-defined natural transformations that satisfy the triangular equalities (
8
) in Appendix
C
.

Theorem 28
.

The functors
\pazocal{L}
and
\pazocal{R}
form an adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{EED}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CBC}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{R}}

(11)

whose unit is
\eta
(
10
) and whose counit, which is a natural isomorphism, is
\varepsilon
(
9
).

Since the counit
\varepsilon
of the adjunction
\pazocal{L}\dashv\pazocal{R}
is actually a natural isomorphism, the functor
\pazocal{R}\colon\mathsf{CBC}\to\mathsf{EED}
is full and faithful.
It turns out, however, that the adjunction
\pazocal{L}\dashv\pazocal{R}
is not an equivalence, because
\pazocal{L}
is not faithful. The following example shows why.

Example 29
.

Let
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
be the signatures in Example
4
and
\mathbb{P}
be some signature of predicate symbols.
Let
LT_{1}\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{1}}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be the indexed Lindenbaum-Tarski algebras for
\Sigma_{1}
defined as in Example
8
.
Recall from Example
4
the strict cartesian functor
Q\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{2}}\to\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{1}}
, mapping
g_{1},g_{2}\in\Sigma_{2}
to
f\in\Sigma_{1}
, and observe that

LT_{1}^{\prime}=LT_{1}\circ Q^{\opposite}\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{2}}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL

is an elementary, existential doctrine by Remark
11
.
Its behaviour is somewhat peculiar: it maps
n\in\mathbb{N}
to the set of formulas
\phi\colon(n,0)
built from
\Sigma_{1}
and
\mathbb{P}
and to any tuple of terms
\langle t_{1},\dots,t_{m}\rangle\colon n\to m
in
\Sigma_{2}
assigns the function mapping a formula
\phi\colon(m,0)
to
\phi[^{Q(t_{1}),\dots,Q(t_{m})}/_{x_{1},\dots,x_{m}}]
. Observe that each
Q(t_{i})
is again a term in
\Sigma_{1}
: the symbols
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are both translated to
f
. Somehow
LT_{1}^{\prime}
behaves like
LT_{1}
but they are different doctrines since their index categories,
\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{2}}
and
\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{1}}
, are not isomorphic. However, when transforming them into cartesian bicategories via
\pazocal{L}
, one obtains that
\pazocal{L}(LT_{1}^{\prime})=\pazocal{L}(LT_{1})
: objects are natural numbers and morphisms
n\to m
are the elements of the set

LT_{1}^{\prime}(n+m)=LT_{1}(Q(n+m))=LT_{1}(Q(n)+Q(m))=LT_{1}(n+m).

To formally show that
\pazocal{L}
is not faithful we now define two morphisms in
\mathsf{EED}
, both from
LT_{1}
to
LT_{1}^{\prime}
, and show that their image along
\pazocal{L}
is the same. Consider the strict cartesian functors
F_{1},F_{2}\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{1}}\to\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{2}}
from Example
4
and observe that

QF_{i}=\id_{\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{1}}}
for
i=1,2
. We are in the following situation:

}
}\quad\text{ which means that }LT_{1}^{\prime}\circ F_{i}^{\opposite}=LT_{1}\circ Q^{\opposite}\circ F_{i}^{\opposite}=LT_{1},

thus
(F_{1},\id_{LT_{1}})
and
(F_{2},\id_{LT_{1}})
are distinct morphisms in
\mathsf{EED}
from
LT_{1}
to
LT_{1}^{\prime}
. Since
\pazocal{L}(LT_{1})=\pazocal{L}(LT_{1}^{\prime})
, it follows from the definition of
\pazocal{L}
that

\pazocal{L}(F_{1},\id)=\id_{\mathscr{A}_{LT}}=\pazocal{L}(F_{2},\id).

8
An Equivalence

In the previous section we identified a doctrine with peculiar behaviour, and used it to show that (
11
) is not an equivalence. Here we characterise the additional constraints on doctrines that are needed for an equivalence.

To make the adjunction (
11
) an equivalence, we need its unit
\eta\colon\id_{\mathsf{EED}}\to\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}
to be a natural isomorphism. This would mean that

\eta_{P}=(\Gamma_{P},P\rho)\colon P\to\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)

ought to be invertible in
\mathsf{EED}
for any elementary existential doctrine
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
. Since

\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)=\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(-,I)\colon\Map(\mathscr{A}_{P})^{\opposite}\to\InfSL,

by definition
\eta_{P}
has an inverse in
\mathsf{EED}
if and only if the functor
\Gamma_{P}\colon\mathbb{C}\to\Map(\mathscr{A}_{P})
is an isomorphism of cartesian categories, in which case
({\Gamma}^{-1}_{P},P{\rho}^{-1})
would be the inverse of
\eta_{P}
.

But
\Gamma_{P}
is the identity on objects: to be an isomorphism, full and faithful suffices. That is, the maps of
\mathscr{A}_{P}
must bijectively correspond
with the arrows of the base category
\mathbb{C}
of
P
. This is not necessarily the case, as arrows of
\mathbb{C}
are not involved in the construction of
\mathscr{A}_{P}
.

The technical tools that allow us to bridge the gap between morphisms in
\mathbb{C}
and maps in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
are provided by
[
24
]
: the next two definitions are equivalent to faithfulness and, respectively, fullness of
\Gamma_{P}
.

Definition 30
.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be an elementary existential doctrine and
\alpha\in P(A)
. A
comprehension
of
\alpha
is an arrow
\{\!|\alpha|\!\}\colon X\to A
in
\mathbb{C}
such that
\top_{PX}\leq P_{\{\!|\alpha|\!\}}(\alpha)
and such that for every
h\colon Z\to A
for which
\top_{PZ}\leq P_{h}(\alpha)
, there is a unique arrow
h^{\prime}\colon Z\to X
such that
h=\{\!|\alpha|\!\}\circ h^{\prime}
.
P

has comprehensive diagonals
if every diagonal arrow
\Delta_{A}\colon A\to A\times A
is the comprehension of
\delta^{P}_{A}
.

Example 31
.

The doctrine
LT_{1}^{\prime}\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{2}}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
from Example
29
does not have comprehensive diagonal. Indeed
\Delta_{1}
is not the comprehension of
\delta_{1}
: take as
h
the arrow
\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle\colon 1\to 2
and observe that
{LT_{1}^{\prime}}_{\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle}(\delta_{1})=\bigl(f(x_{1})=f(x_{1})\bigr)\colon(1,0)
and
\top\colon(1,0)\leq\bigl(f(x_{1})=f(x_{1})\bigr)\colon(1,0)
. Yet there exists no
h^{\prime}\colon 1\to 1
in
\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{2}}
such that
\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle=\Delta_{1}\circ h^{\prime}
.

Definition 32
.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be an elementary, existential doctrine. We say that
P

satisfies the Rule of Unique Choice
(RUC) if for every
R\in P(X\times Y)
which is a map in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
there exists an arrow
f\colon X\to Y
such that

\top_{PX}\leq P_{\langle\id_{X},f\rangle}(R).

Example 33
.

All the doctrines considered so far satisfy RUC. For an example of a doctrine that does not satisfy it consider the composition of
F_{1}^{\opposite}\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{1}}^{\opposite}\to\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{2}}^{\opposite}
(Example
4
) and
LT_{2}\colon\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{2}}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
(Example
8
) that, by Remark
11
, is an elementary existential doctrine. This doctrine maps
n
to the set of formulas
\phi\colon(n,0)
where terms are built from
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
, but the index category
L_{\Sigma_{1}}
contains terms built from
f
that is translated to
g_{1}
by
F_{1}
. Now, the formula
\phi\equiv\bigl(g_{2}(x_{1})=x_{2}\bigr)\colon(2,0)
belongs to
LT_{2}\circ F_{1}^{\opposite}(1+1)
and gives rise to a
map in
\mathscr{A}_{LT_{2}\circ F_{1}^{\opposite}}
, but there is no arrow
t\colon 1\to 1
in
\mathsf{L}_{\Sigma_{1}}
such that
\top\leq{LT_{2}}_{\langle\id,F_{1}(t)\rangle}(\phi)
.

We denote by
\overline{\mathsf{EED}}
the full sub-category of
\mathsf{EED}
consisting only of those elementary existential doctrines with comprehensive diagonals and satisfying the Rule of Unique Choice. Conveniently, it turns out that the image of
\pazocal{R}
is already contained in
\overline{\mathsf{EED}}
.

Proposition 34
.

Let
\mathbb{B}
be a cartesian bicategory. Then
\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})
is in
\overline{\mathsf{EED}}
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}\colon\Map(\mathbb{B})\to\Map(\mathscr{A}_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})})=\Map(\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B}))
is full and faithful. As noticed in Remark
27
,

\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}={\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\Map(\mathbb{B})}.

Since
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}
is an isomorphism in
\mathsf{CBC}
, it is faithful, therefore its restriction
\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
is as well. Moreover,
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}
is full: if
R\colon X\to Y
in
\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})
is a map, then there exists
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{B}
such that
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}(f)=R
. In fact,
f=\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}(R)
and since
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}
is a morphism in
\mathsf{CBC}
, by Proposition
18
we have that
f
is a map in
\mathbb{B}
. Therefore,
\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
is full.
∎

Theorem 35
.

The categories
\overline{\mathsf{EED}}
and
\mathsf{CBC}
are equivalent via adjunction (
11
) where
\pazocal{L}
and
\pazocal{R}
are respectively restricted and corestricted to
\overline{\mathsf{EED}}
.

9
Conclusion

We gave an exhaustive analysis of the relationship between two different categorifications of regular logic: the
universal
approach of elementary existential doctrines, and the
algebraic
approach of cartesian bicategories. We showed that cartesian bicategories give rise to elementary existential doctrines and, expanding a remark in
[
26
]
, that also the other direction is possible. We proved that this correspondence is functorial, in the sense that we have a pair of functors
\pazocal{L}\colon\mathsf{EED}\to\mathsf{CBC}
and
\pazocal{R}\colon\mathsf{CBC}\to\mathsf{EED}
which are moreover adjoint (Theorem
28
).

This adjunction can be strengthened to an equivalence provided that we refine the notion of doctrine, excluding some problematic examples (e.g. Example
29
). These cases lay outside the image of
\pazocal{R}
and thus this restriction does not affect cartesian bicategories (Theorem
35
).

We hope that understanding the relationship between
\mathsf{CBC}
and
\mathsf{EED}
may provide some hints on the nature of the additional algebraic structure needed for cartesian bicategories to capture full first order logic, which one can do on the
\mathsf{EED}
, universal side by considering Lawvere’s original hyperdoctrines. It is probable that the end result will be closely related to Peirce’s existential graphs
[
30
]
, a 19th century proto-string-diagrammatic logical syntax. This direction has already started to be explored, from diverse perspectives, in
[
7
,
6
,
17
]
.

Appendix A
Appendix to §
3

In the following, we will write
\Delta_{A}
or even
A\to AA
for the morphism
\langle\id_{A},\id_{A}\rangle
in a cartesian category. Also, projections
\pi_{i}\colon A_{1}\times\dots\times A_{n}\to A_{i}
or pairings of them
\langle\pi_{i},\pi_{j}\rangle\colon A_{1}\times\dots\times A_{n}\to A_{i}\times A_{j}
will simply be written as
A_{1}\dots A_{n}\to A_{i}
or
A_{1}\dots A_{n}\to A_{i}A_{j}
respectively. For example,
\pi_{2}\colon A\times B\times C\to B
will be denoted as
ABC\to B
, and
\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{3}\rangle\colon A\times B\times C\to A\times C
as
ABC\to AC
. In particular, the symmetry in a cartesian category
\sigma_{A,B}=\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{1}\rangle\colon A\times B\to B\times A
will be written as
AB\to BA
.

Details on the Elementary Existential Doctrine
\pazocal P\colon\Set^{\opposite}\to\InfSL

The fact that
\pazocal P\colon\Set^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
is an elementary existential doctrine is well-known and the computations to check it are rather easy. Anyway, we think that it might be useful for some readers to report such computations hereafter.

It is convenient to start with (
3
), even if this property is entailed by Definition
5
(see Remark
6
).
Recall that, for
f\colon Y\to X
in
\Set
,
\pazocal P_{f}\colon\pazocal P(X)\to\pazocal P(Y)
is defined as
\pazocal P_{f}(Z)=\{y\in Y\mid f(y)\in Z\}
, in particular for a set
A
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal P(A\times A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal P(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{a\in A\mid(a,a)\in Q\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal P_{\Delta_{A}}}

Then the function
\exists_{\Delta_{A}}\colon\pazocal P(A)\to\pazocal P(A\times A)
defined as in (
3
), namely for all
B\subseteq A

\displaystyle\exists_{\Delta_{A}}(B)

\displaystyle=\pazocal P_{\pi_{1}}(B)\land\delta_{A}

\displaystyle=\{(x,b)\mid b\in B\}\cap\{(a,a)\mid a\in A\}

\displaystyle=\{(b,b)\mid b\in B\}

is in fact the left adjoint of
\pazocal P_{\Delta_{A}}
: indeed for
B\in\pazocal P(A)
and
Q\in\pazocal P(A\times A)

\exists_{\Delta_{A}}(B)\subseteq Q\iff\forall b\in B\ldotp(b,b)\in Q\iff B\subseteq\pazocal P_{\Delta_{A}}(Q).

Similarly, for
e\colon X\times A\to X\times A\times A
given by
e(x,a)=(x,a,a)
, the function
\exists_{e}\colon\pazocal P(X\times A)\to\pazocal P(X\times A\times A)
defined as in (
1
):

\displaystyle\exists_{e}(S)

\displaystyle=\pazocal P_{\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle}(S)\land\pazocal P_{\langle\pi_{2},\pi 3\rangle}(\delta_{A})

\displaystyle=\{(x,a,b)\mid(x,a)\in S\}\cap\{(x,a,b)\mid(a,b)\in\delta_{A}\}

\displaystyle=\{(x,a,a)\mid(x,a)\in S\}

is easily seen to be left adjoint of
\pazocal P_{e}\colon\pazocal P(X\times A\times A)\to\pazocal P(X\times A)
. Moreover, for
\pi\colon X\times A\to A
,
S\subseteq X\times A
and
B\subseteq A
,

\displaystyle\exists_{\pi}(S)\subseteq B

\displaystyle\iff\forall a\in A\ldotp\bigl(\exists x\in X\ldotp(x,a)\in S\implies a\in B\bigr)

\displaystyle\iff\forall(x,a)\in S\ldotp a\in B

\displaystyle\iff S\subseteq\pazocal P_{\pi}(B)

so
\exists_{\pi}
is left adjoint to
\pazocal P_{\pi}
.

Regarding the Beck-Chevalley condition: if
f\colon A^{\prime}\to A
and
\pi\colon X\times A\to A
, then the pullback of
f
against
\pi
is:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{X}\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

Take
S\in\pazocal P(X\times A)
. Then
\pazocal P_{\id_{X}\times f}(S)=\{(x,a^{\prime})\mid(x,f(a^{\prime}))\in S\}
, therefore

\displaystyle\exists_{\pi^{\prime}}(\pazocal P_{\id_{X}\times f}(S))

\displaystyle=\{a^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}\mid\exists x\in X\ldotp(x,a^{\prime})\in\pazocal P_{\id_{X}\times f}(S)\}

\displaystyle=\{a^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}\mid\exists x\in X\ldotp(x,f(a^{\prime}))\in S\}

while

\displaystyle\pazocal P_{f}(\exists_{\pi}(S))

\displaystyle=\{a^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}\mid f(a^{\prime})\in\pazocal P_{f}(\exists_{\pi}(S))\}

\displaystyle=\{a^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}\mid\exists x\in X\ldotp(x,f(a^{\prime}))\in S\}

Hence the Beck-Chevalley condition is satisfied.
Finally, for
\pi\colon X\times A\to A
,
B\in\pazocal P(A)
and
S\in\pazocal P(X\times A)
:

\displaystyle\exists_{\pi}(\pazocal P_{\pi}(B)\land S)

\displaystyle=\exists_{\pi}\bigl(\{(x,b)\in X\times A\mid b\in B\}\cap S\bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{\pi}\bigl(\{(x,b)\in S\mid b\in B\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\{b\in B\mid\exists x\in X\ldotp(x,b)\in S\}

\displaystyle=B\cap\{a\in A\mid\exists x\in X\ldotp(x,a)\in S\}

\displaystyle=B\land\exists_{\pi}(S)

therefore also the Frobenius reciprocity is satisfied.

Additional General Facts about Elementary Existential Doctrines

We report a few well known, simple results that are going to be useful later on.

Lemma 36
.

Let
P
be an existential, elementary doctrine. Then
\delta_{A}=\exists_{\Delta_{A}}(\top)
. Moreover,

\delta_{A\times B}=P_{ABAB\to AA}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{ABAB\to BB}(\delta_{B})

for all objects
A
and
B
.

Proof.

By (
3
), we have

\exists_{\Delta}(\top)=P_{\pi_{1}}(\top)\wedge\delta_{A}=\top\wedge\delta_{A}=\delta_{A}.

As for the second part of the statement:
since the projection
ABAB\to AA
coincides with the composite
ABAB\to AAB\to AA
in
\mathbb{C}
, by functoriality of
P
and the fact that
P_{f}
preserves meets for any morphism
f
in
\mathbb{C}
we have

\displaystyle P_{ABAB\to AA}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{ABAB\to BB}(\delta_{B})

\displaystyle=P_{ABAB\to AABB}\Bigl(P_{AABB\to AAB}\bigl(P_{AAB\to AA}(\delta_{A})\bigr)\wedge P_{AABB\to BB}(\delta_{B})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{ABAB\to AABB}\Bigl(\exists_{e}\bigl(P_{AAB\to AA}(\delta_{A})\bigr)\Bigr)

where
e=\id_{A}\times\id_{A}\times\Delta_{B}
, due to (
1
). Notice that the morphism
\id_{A}\times\sigma_{B,A}\times\id_{B}
is an isomorphism, therefore
P_{ABAB\to AABB}
is the left adjoint of its inverse. Thus we write
P_{ABAB\to AABB}=\exists_{AABB\to ABAB}
and we have:

\displaystyle\exists_{AABB\to ABAB}\exists_{e}\bigl(P_{AAB\to AA}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{AAB\to AB}(\top)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{AABB\to ABAB}\exists_{e}\exists_{e^{\prime}}(\top)

where
e^{\prime}=\Delta_{A}\times\id_{B}
. Since left adjoints compose and
{e^{\prime}}\mathrel{;}e\mathrel{;}(\id_{A}\times\sigma\times\id_{B})=\Delta_{A\times B}
, we obtain

\displaystyle P_{ABAB\to AA}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{ABAB\to BB}(\delta_{B})

\displaystyle=\exists_{\Delta_{A\times B}}(\top)

\displaystyle=\delta_{A\times B}.\qed

Proposition 37
.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\Poset
be any functor. Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

be a commutative diagram such that
P_{f}
has a left-adjoint
\exists_{f}
and likewise for
k
. Then

\exists_{f}(P_{h}(\gamma))\leq P_{g}(\exists_{k}(\gamma))

for any
\gamma\in P(C)
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\in P(C)
. Then
\exists_{f}(P_{h}(\gamma))\leq P_{g}(\exists_{k}(\gamma))
if and only if

P_{h}(\gamma)\leq P_{f}\bigl(P_{g}(\exists_{k}(\gamma))\bigr)=P_{h}\bigl(P_{k}(\exists_{k}(\gamma))\bigr)

which is always true because
\gamma\leq P_{k}(\exists_{k}(\gamma))
and
P_{h}
is monotone.
∎

Combining Lemma
36
and Proposition
37
we obtain the following simple result.

Corollary 38
.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be an existential elementary doctrine and
I
be a terminal object in
\mathbb{C}
. Then for all
X

P_{{}!\colon X\to I\times I}(\delta_{I})=\top_{P(X)}.

Appendix B
Appendix to §
6

Here we show that the graph functor
\Gamma_{P}
of an elementary existential doctrine
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
is indeed functorial (Lemma
40
) and that
\Gamma_{P}(f)
has a right adjoint in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
for every arrow
f
in
\mathbb{C}
(Lemma
42
).

Proof of Proposition
23

Proposition 39
.

The symmetry
\sigma_{A,B}\colon A\times B\to B\times A
of the cartesian product on
\mathbb{C}
induces a bijection

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(X,Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(Y,X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\sigma_{Y,X}}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet^{\opposite}}

This is contravariant with respect to composition in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
, meaning that

g^{\opposite}\mathrel{;}f^{\opposite}=(f\mathrel{;}g)^{\opposite}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta_{X}^{\opposite}=\delta_{X}.

Proof.

For
f\colon X\to Y
, we define
f^{\opposite}=P_{\sigma_{Y,X}}(f)
, where
\sigma_{Y,X}\colon Y\times X\to X\times Y
is the symmetry of
\mathbb{C}
.

Consider also
g\colon Y\to Z
. Then

(f\mathrel{;}g)^{\opposite}=P_{\sigma_{Z,X}}\Bigl(\exists_{XYZ\to XZ}\bigl(P_{XYZ\to XY}(f)\wedge P_{XYZ\to YZ}(g)\bigr)\Bigr)

while

\displaystyle g^{\opposite}\mathrel{;}f^{\opposite}

\displaystyle=\exists_{ZYX\to ZX}\Bigl(P_{ZYX\to ZY}\bigl(P_{\sigma_{Z,Y}}(g)\bigr)\wedge P_{ZYX\to YX}\bigl(P_{\sigma_{Y,X}}(f)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{ZYX\to ZX}\Bigl(P_{ZYX\to XYZ}\bigl(P_{XYZ\to YZ}(g)\wedge P_{XYZ\to XY}(f)\bigr)\Bigr)

which is equal to
(f\mathrel{;}g)^{\opposite}
thanks to the Beck-Chevalley condition applied to the pullback:

\hbox to147.84pt{\vbox to54.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 78.2707pt\lower-23.9861pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-69.56924pt}{-19.49306pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
