Lemma 40
.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be an elementary existential doctrine.
There is a functor
\Gamma_{P}\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathscr{A}_{P}
, called the
graph functor of
P
, defined to be the identity on objects and on morphisms defined by sending
f\colon X\to Y
to

\Gamma_{P}(f)=P_{f\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y})\in P(X\times Y)=\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(X,Y).

Proof.

Firstly
\Gamma_{P}
preserves identities because
\Gamma_{P}(\id_{X})=P_{\id_{X}\times\id_{X}}(\delta_{X})=\delta_{X}
. Therefore it remains to show that
\Gamma_{P}
preserves
composition. Let
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to Z
be morphisms in
\mathbb{C}
.
Consider the projections

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}

Then

\Gamma_{P}(f)\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(g)=\exists_{\pi_{Y}}(P_{\pi_{Z}}(P_{f\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y}))\wedge P_{\pi_{X}}(P_{g\times\id_{Z}}(\delta_{Z}))).

Now the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{Y}\times\id_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{Y}}

commutes and therefore

P_{\pi_{Z}}(P_{f\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y}))=P_{f\times\id_{Y}\times\id_{Z}}(P_{\pi}(\delta_{Y}))

and likewise

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{Y}\times\id_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\prime}}

commutes, where
\pi^{\prime}
projects onto the second and third component.
Therefore we also have

P_{\pi_{X}}(P_{g\times\id_{Z}}(\delta_{Z})))=P_{f\times\id_{Y}\times\id_{Z}}(P_{\pi^{\prime}}(P_{g\times\id_{Z}}(\delta_{Y}))).

This gives us

\Gamma_{P}(f)\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(g)=\exists_{\pi_{Y}}(P_{f\times\id_{Y}\times\id_{Z}}(P_{\pi}(\delta_{Y})\wedge P_{\pi^{\prime}}(P_{g\times\id_{Z}}(\delta_{Y}))).

Let
e=\Delta_{Y}\times\id_{Z}\colon Y\times Z\to Y\times Y\times Z
, then by (
1
), we have

\Gamma_{P}(f)\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(g)=\exists_{\pi_{Y}}(P_{f\times\id_{Y}\times\id_{Z}}(\exists_{e}(P_{g\times\id_{Z}}(\delta_{Z})))).

Using the Beck-Chevalley condition with the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y\times Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{Y}\times\id_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

where
\rho
projects onto the first and third component, this becomes

\Gamma_{P}(f)\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(g)=P_{f\times\id_{Z}}(\exists_{\rho}(\exists_{e}(P_{g\times\id_{Z}}(\delta_{Z}))))

Now since
e\mathrel{;}\rho=\id
and by uniqueness of adjoints, this becomes

\Gamma_{P}(f)\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(g)=P_{f\times\id_{Z}}(P_{g\times\id_{Z}}(\delta_{Z}))=P_{(f\mathrel{;}g)\times\id_{Z}}(\delta_{Z})=\Gamma_{P}(f\mathrel{;}g).\qed

Remark 41
.

It follow immediately from the definitions of
\Gamma_{P}
and of tensor product in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
that for any
f,g
in
\mathbb{C}
,
\Gamma_{P}(f\times g)=\Gamma_{P}(g)\otimes\Gamma_{P}(g)
.

Lemma 42
.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be an elementary, existential doctrine,
\Gamma_{P}\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathscr{A}_{P}
the functor from Lemma
40
.
For any morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{C}
,
\Gamma_{P}(f)^{\opposite}
is right-adjoint to
\Gamma_{P}(f)
, that is

\delta_{X}\leq\Gamma_{P}(f)\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(f)^{\opposite}\quad\text{and}\quad\Gamma_{P}(f)^{\opposite}\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(f)\leq\delta_{Y}.

Proof.

It is easy to check that
\Gamma_{P}(f)^{\opposite}=P_{\id\times f}(\delta_{Y})
.

•

We start by proving
\delta_{X}\leq\Gamma_{P}(f)\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(f)^{\opposite}
. Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2,3}}

be the projections. Then

\displaystyle\Gamma_{P}(f)\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(f)^{\opposite}

\displaystyle=\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}(P_{\pi_{1,2}}(P_{f\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y}))\wedge P_{\pi_{2,3}}(P_{\id_{Y}\times f}(\delta_{Y})))

\displaystyle=\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}(P_{f\times\id_{Y}\times f}(P_{\pi_{1,2}}(\delta_{Y})\wedge P_{\pi_{2,3}}(\delta_{Y}))

\displaystyle=\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}(P_{f\times\id_{Y}\times f}(\exists_{e}(\delta_{Y}))

where the last step uses (
1
) with
e=\Delta_{Y}\times\id_{Y}
.
Applying Beck-Chevalley using the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times\id\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,3}}

yields

\Gamma_{P}(f)\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(f)^{\opposite}=P_{f\times f}(\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}(\exists_{e}(\delta_{Y})))=P_{f\times f}(\delta_{Y})

where
\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}\circ\exists_{e}=\id
.
Now applying Lemma
36
and Proposition
37
yields

\Gamma_{P}(f)\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(f)^{\opposite}=P_{f\times f}(\exists_{\Delta}(\top))\geq\exists_{\Delta}(P_{f}(\top))=\exists_{\Delta}(\top)=\delta_{Y}

•

It remains to prove that
\Gamma_{P}(f)^{\opposite}\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(f)\leq\delta_{Y}
. Let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2,3}}

be the projections. Then

\displaystyle\Gamma_{P}(f)^{\opposite}\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(f)

\displaystyle=\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}(P_{\pi_{1,2}}(P_{\id_{Y}\times f}(\delta_{Y}))\wedge P_{\pi_{2,3}}(P_{f\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y})))

\displaystyle=\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}(P_{\id_{Y}\times f\times\id_{Y}}(P_{\pi_{1,2}}(\delta_{Y})\wedge P_{\pi_{2,3}}(\delta_{Y}))

\displaystyle=\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}(P_{\id_{Y}\times f\times\id_{Y}}(\exists_{e}(\delta_{Y}))

where the last step uses (
1
) with
e=\Delta_{Y}\times\id_{Y}
.
Using Proposition
37
with the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\times f\times\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,3}}

we get

\Gamma_{P}(f)^{\opposite}\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(f)\leq\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}(\exists_{e}(\delta_{Y}))=\delta_{Y}.\qed

Proof of Theorem
25

Here we prove that
\mathscr{A}_{P}
is a cartesian bicategory (Theorem
45
) and that the assignment
P\mapsto\mathscr{A}_{P}
extends to a functor
\pazocal{L}\colon\mathsf{EED}\to\mathsf{CBC}
(Proposition
46
).

Theorem 43
.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be an existential, elementary doctrine. Then
\mathscr{A}_{P}
is a poset-enriched
category.

Proof.

That
\delta_{X}
serves as the identity of composition follows easily from (
1
):
Let
f\in\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(X,Y)=P(X\times Y)
and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2,3}}

the projections. Then

f\mathrel{;}\delta_{Y}=\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}(P_{\pi_{1,2}}(f)\wedge P_{\pi_{2,3}}(\delta_{Y})).

By (
1
), we have
P_{\pi_{1,2}}(f)\wedge P_{\pi_{2,3}}(\delta_{Y})=\exists_{e}(f)
where

e=\id_{X}\times\Delta_{Y}\colon X\times Y\to X\times Y\times Y
.
Therefore,
f\mathrel{;}\delta_{Y}=\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}(\exists_{e}(f))
. Since left-adjoints compose, we have that
\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}\circ\exists_{e}

is left-adjoint to
P_{\pi_{1,3}\circ e}=P_{\id}=\id
. By uniqueness of adjoints, also
\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}\circ\exists_{e}
is the identity and hence

f\mathrel{;}\delta_{Y}=\exists_{\pi_{1,3}}(\exists_{e}(f))=f.

That also
\delta_{X}\mathrel{;}f=f
follows by Proposition
39
.

To see that composition is associative, let
f\colon A\to B,g\colon B\to C,h\colon C\to D
.
We have

f\mathrel{;}g=\exists_{ABC\to AC}(P_{ABC\to AB}(f)\land P_{ABC\to BC}(g)).

Let us give the inner expression a name, and let
\alpha=P_{ABC\to AB}(f)\land P_{ABC\to BC}(g)
so that

f\mathrel{;}g=\exists_{ABC\to AC}(\alpha).

Then

\displaystyle(f\mathrel{;}g)\mathrel{;}h

\displaystyle=\exists_{ACD\to AD}(P_{ACD\to AC}(f\mathrel{;}g)\land P_{ACD\to CD}(h))

\displaystyle=\exists_{ACD\to AD}(P_{ACD\to AC}(\exists_{ABC\to AC}(\alpha)\land P_{ACD\to CD}(h))).

Now we can use the Beck-Chevalley condition with the following square of projections:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times B\times C\times D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times C\times D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times B\times C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times C}

to infer that

P_{{ACD\to AC}}(\exists_{ABC\to AC}(\alpha))=\exists_{ABCD\to ACD}(P_{ABCD\to ABC}(\alpha))

and therefore

(f\mathrel{;}g)\mathrel{;}h=\exists_{ACD\to AD}(\exists_{ABCD\to ACD}(P_{{ABCD\to ABC}}(\alpha))\wedge P_{{ACD\to CD}}(h)).

Using Frobenius reciprocity to get everything into the existential quantifier we get

(f\mathrel{;}g)\mathrel{;}h=\exists_{{ACD\to AD}}(\exists_{{ABCD\to ACD}}(P_{{ABCD\to ABC}}(\alpha)\wedge P_{{ABCD\to CD}}(h)))

and therefore by uniqueness of adjoints also

(f\mathrel{;}g)\mathrel{;}h=\exists_{{ABCD\to AD}}(P_{{ABCD\to ABC}}(\alpha)\wedge P_{{ABCD\to CD}}(h)).

Now using the definition of
\alpha
and the fact that any
P_{\pi}
preserves meet, this expands into

(f\mathrel{;}g)\mathrel{;}h=\exists_{{ABCD\to AD}}(P_{{ABCD\to AB}}(f)\wedge P_{{ABCD\to BC}}(g)\wedge P_{{ABCD\to CD}}(h)).

Completely analogously one can prove that also

f\mathrel{;}(g\mathrel{;}h)=\exists_{{ABCD\to AD}}(P_{{ABCD\to AB}}(f)\wedge P_{{ABCD\to BC}}(g)\wedge P_{{ABCD\to CD}}(h))

thus composition is therefore associative.

Finally, composition is a monotonous operation because
P_{\pi}
and
\exists_{\pi}
are monotonous functions, thus if
f_{1}\leq f_{2}
then
f_{1};g\leq f_{2};g
:
therefore the category
\mathscr{A}_{P}
is poset-enriched.∎

Proposition 44
.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be an existential, elementary doctrine. Then
\mathscr{A}_{P}
is a symmetric monoidal category.

Proof.

We first prove that
\otimes
is a functor. Let
f_{1}\colon A\to B
,
g_{1}\colon C\to D
,
f_{2}\colon B\to E
,
g_{2}\colon D\to F
. We need to show that

(f_{1}\otimes g_{1})\mathrel{;}(f_{2}\otimes g_{2})=(f_{1}\mathrel{;}f_{2})\otimes(g_{1}\mathrel{;}g_{2}).

Starting from the left-hand side:

\displaystyle{\bigl(P_{ACBD\to AB}(f_{1})\wedge P_{ACBD\to CD}(g_{1})\bigr)}\mathrel{;}{\bigl(P_{BDEF\to BE}(f_{2})\wedge P_{BDEF\to DF}(g_{2})\bigr)}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle\exists_{ACBDEF\to ACEF}\Bigl(P_{ACBDEF\to ACBD}\bigl(P_{ACBD\to AB}(f_{1})\wedge P_{ACBD\to CD}(g_{1})\bigr)

\displaystyle\wedge P_{ACBDEF\to BDEF}\bigl(P_{BDEF\to BE}(f_{2})\wedge P_{BDEF\to DF}(g_{2})\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle\exists_{ACBDEF\to ACEF}\bigl(P_{ACBDEF\to AB}(f_{1})\wedge P_{ACBDEF\to CD}(g_{1})

\displaystyle\wedge P_{ACBDEF\to BE}(f_{2})\wedge P_{ACBDEF}(g_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle\exists_{ACBDEF\to ACEF}\Bigl(P_{ACBDEF\to ABE}\bigl(P_{ABE\to AB}(f_{1})\wedge P_{ABE\to BE}(f_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle\wedge P_{ACBDEF\to CDF}\bigl(P_{CDF\to CD}(g_{1})\wedge P_{CDF\to DF}(g_{2})\bigr)\Bigr).

Let us call, for the sake of brevity,

\alpha=P_{ABE\to AB}(f_{1})\wedge P_{ABE\to BE}(f_{2})\text{ and }\beta=P_{CDF\to CD}(g_{1})\wedge P_{CDF\to DF}(g_{2}).

Then we proved that

(f_{1}\otimes g_{1})\mathrel{;}(f_{2}\otimes g_{2})=\exists_{ACBDEF\to ACEF}\bigl(P_{ACBDEF\to ABE}(\alpha)\wedge P_{ACBDEF\to CDF}(\beta)\bigr).

We notice that the projection
ACBDEF\to ACEF
, over which we compute the existential quantifier, can be performed in two steps: first we forget
D
, then
B
. Similarly, the projection
ACBDEF\to ABE
, whose image along
P
we calculate in
\alpha
, can be decomposed in two steps. We use this to put ourselves in the position of applying the Frobenius reciprocity property of
P
:

\displaystyle\exists_{ACBDEF\to ACEF}\bigl(P_{ACBDEF\to ABE}(\alpha)\wedge P_{ACBDEF\to CDF}(\beta)\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exists_{ACBEF\to ACEF}

\displaystyle\exists_{ACBDEF\to ACBEF}\Bigl(P_{ACBDEF\to ACBEF}\bigl(P_{ACBEF\to ABE}(\alpha)\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 90.00014pt\wedge P_{ACBDEF\to CDF}(\beta)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exists_{ACBEF\to ACEF}\Bigl(P_{ACBEF\to ABE}(\alpha)

\displaystyle\hskip 75.0001pt\wedge\exists_{ACBDEF\to ACBEF}\bigl(P_{ACBDEF\to CDF}(\beta)\bigr)\Bigr).

Now we use the Beck-Chevalley condition on the following pullback, consisting only of projections:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ACBDEF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ACBEF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CDF}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CF}

resulting in exchanging the order of
\exists
and
P
, obtaining:

\exists_{ACBEF\to ACEF}\Bigl(P_{ACBEF\to CF}\bigl(\exists_{CDF\to CF}(\beta)\bigr)\wedge P_{ACBEF\to ABE}(\alpha)\Bigr).

By writing the projection
ACBEF\to CF
as a the composite

ACBEF\to ACEF\to CF,

one can again use the Frobenius reciprocity and then the Beck-Chevalley condition to finally obtain

P_{ACEF\to CF}\bigl(\exists_{CDF\to CF}(\beta)\bigr)\wedge P_{ACEF\to AE}\bigl(\exists_{ABE\to AE}(\alpha)\bigr)

which is equal, by definition, to
(f_{1}\mathrel{;}f_{2})\otimes(g_{1}\mathrel{;}g_{2})
, as required. Thus
\otimes
preserves compositions; preservation of identities, which is tantamount to

P_{ABAB\to AA}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{ABAB\to BB}(\delta_{B})=\delta_{A\times B},

follows from Lemma
36
. Therefore
\otimes
is a functor.

Next, we show that
\otimes
is coherently associative, unitary and commutative.
We have already noticed in Remark
41
that the functor
\Gamma_{P}
from Lemma
40
transports the product functor of
\mathbb{C}
into the tensor functor
\otimes
of
\mathscr{A}_{P}
: this suggests to transfer the whole cartesian structure of
\mathbb{C}
into
\mathscr{A}_{P}
, thus the associator, the unitors and the symmetry for
\otimes
are defined as the image of their correspondent isomorphisms in
\mathbb{C}
. This automatically ensures that they are all natural isomorphisms (being whiskerings of a functor with natural isomorphisms) coherent with
\otimes
.
∎

Theorem 45
.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be an elementary existential doctrine. Then
\mathscr{A}_{P}
is a cartesian bicategory.

Proof.

By Theorem
43
and Proposition
44
,
\mathscr{A}_{P}
is a poset-enriched, symmetric monoidal category.
Since
\mathbb{C}
is a cartesian category, every object is canonically equipped with a comonoid given by copying and discarding.
We can use the functor
\Gamma_{P}\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathscr{A}_{P}
to transport
this comonoid to
\mathscr{A}_{P}
and Lemma
42
shows that copying and discarding both have right-adjoints.
Since the comonoids in
\mathbb{C}
are coherent with the monoidal structure, the same is true in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
as the structure is transported through the
strict monoidal functor
\Gamma_{P}
.
It therefore remains to prove that every morphism is a lax-comonoid homomorphism and that the Frobenius law holds.
To see that every morphism is a lax-comonoid homomorphism, we need to prove that

R
\scriptstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{X}
R
R
\leq
\scriptstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{Y}

and

\leq
R
\scriptstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{X}

(12)

for any morphism
R\in\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(X,Y)=P(X\times Y)
.
The latter is very easy to see because the right-hand side is the top element in
P(X\times I)
, see Corollary
38
. So we focus on the former inequality.

We start with the left-hand side.
In order to distinguish among the three occurrences of
Y
in the composite of
R
with the diagonal in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
, we will write
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
and
Y_{3}
, even though they all represent the same object
Y
of
\mathscr{A}_{P}
. We have:

\displaystyle\exists_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to XY_{2}Y_{3}}\Bigl(P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to XY_{1}}(R)\wedge P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}}\bigl(P_{\Delta_{Y}Y_{2}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y^{2}})\bigr)\Bigr)

and since
\delta_{Y^{2}}=P_{\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{3}\rangle}(\delta_{Y})\wedge P_{\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{4}\rangle}(\delta_{Y})
by Lemma
36
, we get

\displaystyle\exists_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to XY_{2}Y_{3}}\bigl(P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to XY_{1}}(R)\wedge P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to Y_{1}Y_{2}}(\delta_{Y})\wedge P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to Y_{2}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to XY_{2}Y_{3}}\Bigl(P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to XY_{1}Y_{2}}\bigl(P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}\to XY_{1}}(R)\wedge P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}\to Y_{1}Y_{2}}(\delta_{Y})\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 90.00014pt\wedge P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to Y_{1}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y})\Bigr).

Using (
1
), the symmetry in
\mathbb{C}
and (
1
) again, we obtain:

\displaystyle\exists_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to XY_{2}Y_{3}}\Bigl(P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to XY_{1}Y_{2}}\bigl(\exists_{X\Delta_{Y}}(R)\bigr)\wedge P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to Y_{1}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to XY_{2}Y_{3}}\Bigl[P_{X\sigma Y}\Bigl(P_{XY_{2}Y_{1}Y_{3}\to XY_{2}Y_{1}}\bigl(P_{X\sigma}(\exists_{X\Delta_{Y}}(R))\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 120.00018pt\wedge P_{XY_{2}Y_{1}Y_{3}\to Y_{1}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y})\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exists_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to XY_{2}Y_{3}}\Bigl[P_{X\sigma Y}\Bigl(\exists_{XY\Delta_{Y}}\bigl(P_{X\sigma}(\exists_{X\Delta_{Y}}(R))\bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr].

Since
P_{X\sigma Y}
and
P_{X\sigma}
are isomorphisms, they are left adjoints (to their inverses): by composing all the left adjoints in the above expression we obtain

\exists_{X\times\Delta_{Y}}(R).

The right-hand side, instead, is

\displaystyle\exists_{XX^{2}Y^{2}\to XY^{2}}\Bigl(P_{XX^{2}Y^{2}\to XX^{2}}\bigl(P_{\Delta_{X}\times X^{2}}(\delta_{X^{2}})\bigr)\wedge P_{XX^{2}Y^{2}\to X^{2}Y^{2}}(R\otimes R)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{XX^{2}Y^{2}\to XY^{2}}\Bigl(P_{\Delta_{X}\times X^{2}\times Y^{2}}\bigl(P_{X^{2}X^{2}Y^{2}\to X^{2}X^{2}}(\delta_{X^{2}})\wedge P_{X^{2}X^{2}Y^{2}\to X^{2}Y^{2}}(R\otimes R)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{XX^{2}Y^{2}\to XY^{2}}\Bigl(P_{\Delta_{X}\times X^{2}\times Y^{2}}\bigl(\exists_{\Delta_{X^{2}}\times Y^{2}}(R\otimes R)\bigr).\Bigr)

Using the Beck-Chevalley condition on the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces XX^{2}Y^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces XY^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{2}X^{2}Y^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{2}Y^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{3}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{X}\times X^{2}\times Y^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{X}\times Y^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{3}\rangle}

we get

\displaystyle P_{\Delta_{X}\times Y^{2}}\bigl(\exists_{\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{3}\rangle}\exists_{\Delta_{X^{2}\times Y^{2}}}(R\otimes R)\bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{\Delta_{X}\times Y^{2}}(R\otimes R).

Using that

R\otimes R=P_{\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{3}\rangle}(R)\wedge P_{\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{4}\rangle}(R)

we have

P_{\Delta_{X}\times Y^{2}}(R\otimes R)=P_{X\times\pi_{1}\colon X\times Y^{2}\to X\times Y}(R)\wedge P_{X\times\pi_{2}\colon X\times Y^{2}\to X\times Y}(R)

Since we have established the right-hand side as a meet, it suffices to prove that

\exists_{X\times\Delta_{Y}}(R)\leq P_{X\times\pi_{i}}(R)

for
i\in\{1,2\}
and since

P_{X\times\Delta_{Y}}(P_{X\times\pi_{i}}(R))=R

this follows by virtue of
\exists_{X\times\Delta_{Y}}
being a left-adjoint.

Lastly, we need to check the Frobenius law. For that it suffices to prove that

\scriptstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{A}
=

which are elements of
P(A^{4})
.
In the following we will calculate in detail the left-hand side of the equation above, where there are seven occurrences of the same object
A
. For notational convenience, we want to distinguish them, like this:

\scriptstyle{A_{3}}
\scriptstyle{A_{1}}
\scriptstyle{A_{4}}
\scriptstyle{A_{5}}
\scriptstyle{A_{7}}
\scriptstyle{A_{6}}
\scriptstyle{A_{2}}

To name a projection out of a product of copies of
A
, we will use the subscripts to specify which factors we are projecting onto: for example,
\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3}\rangle\colon A^{7}\to A_{1}\times A_{2}\times A_{3}
will simply be denoted as
1234567\to 123
.

By simply unravelling the definition of composition and tensor, and using the fact that
\delta_{A\times A}=\delta_{A}\otimes\delta_{A}
(Lemma
36
), it is easy to see that the left-hand side is equal to:

\displaystyle\exists_{1234567\to 1267}\bigl(

\displaystyle P_{1234567\to 13}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{1234567\to 14}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{1234567\to 47}(\delta_{A})

\displaystyle\wedge P_{1234567\to 57}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{1234567\to 25}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{1234567\to 36}(\delta_{A})\bigr)

which can be rewritten, using (
1
), as

\displaystyle\exists_{1234567\to 1267}\Bigl(

\displaystyle P_{1234567\to 136}\bigl(\exists_{A\times\Delta_{A}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)\wedge P_{1234567\to 147}\bigl(\exists_{A\times\Delta_{A}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)

\displaystyle\wedge P_{1234567\to 257}\bigl(\exists_{A\times\Delta_{A}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)\Bigr).

The projection
1234567\to 1267
of the existential quantifier can be decomposed in three projections which forget only one instance of
A
at a time. Using this and rearranging the expression above, we can put ourselves in the position of being able to use the Frobenius reciprocity as follows: the above is equal to

\displaystyle\exists_{12567\to 1267}\exists_{124567\to 12567}

\displaystyle\exists_{1234567\to 124567}\Bigl[P_{1234567\to 124567}\Bigl(P_{124567\to 147}\bigl(\exists_{A\times\Delta_{A}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 145.00021pt\wedge P_{124567\to 257}\bigl(\exists_{A\times\Delta_{A}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 70.0001pt\wedge P_{1234567\to 136}\bigl(\exists_{A\times\Delta_{A}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exists_{12567\to 1267}

\displaystyle\exists_{124567\to 12567}\Bigl[P_{124567\to 147}\bigl(\exists_{A\times\Delta_{A}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)\wedge P_{124567\to 257}\bigl(\exists_{A\times\Delta_{A}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt\wedge\exists_{1234567\to 124567}\Bigl(P_{1234567\to 136}\bigl(\exists_{A\times\Delta_{A}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr]

By the Beck-Chevalley condition applied to the following pullback, made just of projections:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1234567}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 124567}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 136}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 16}

we have that

\displaystyle\exists_{1234567\to 124567}\Bigl(P_{1234567\to 136}\bigl(\exists_{A\times\Delta_{A}}(\delta_{A})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{124567\to 16}\Bigl(\exists_{136\to 16}\bigl(\exists_{A\times\Delta_{A}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{124567\to 16}(\delta_{A}).

Using this fact and a similar strategy as before, we can reduce the left-hand side to

P_{1267\to 16}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{1267\to 17}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{1267\to 27}(\delta_{A}).

We can use symmetries in
\mathbb{C}
to arrange the above expression so that we can use (
1
) in the following:

\displaystyle P_{1267\to 16}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{1267\to 17}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{1267\to 27}(\delta_{A})

\displaystyle=P_{1267\to 2716}\Bigl[P_{2716\to 271}\Bigl(P_{271\to 27}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{271\to 71}\bigl(P_{\sigma}(\delta_{A})\bigr)\Bigr)\wedge P_{2716\to 16}(\delta_{A})\Bigr]

\displaystyle=P_{1267\to 2716}\Bigl[\exists_{A^{2}\times\Delta_{A}}\Bigl(\exists_{A\times\Delta_{A}}\bigl(\exists_{\Delta_{A}}(\top)\bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr]

Now,
P_{1267\to 2716}
is the left-adjoint of its inverse, which composed to the other existential quantifiers yields simply

\exists_{\langle\id_{A},\id_{A},\id_{A},\id_{A}\rangle}(\top).

Proposition 46
.

The assignment
P\mapsto\mathscr{A}_{P}
extends to a functor
\pazocal{L}\colon\mathsf{EED}\to\mathsf{CBC}
as follows.

•

On objects: for
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
,
\pazocal{L}(P)=\mathscr{A}_{P}
.

•

On morphisms: for
P
as above,
R\colon\mathbb{D}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
and
(F,b)\colon P\to R
in
\mathsf{EED}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}(F,b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times Y)\ni r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{X\times Y}(r)\in R(F(X)\times F(Y))}

Proof.

First we check that
\pazocal{L}(F,b)
is a morphism of cartesian bicategories. Let
X
be an object of
\mathbb{C}
: the identity morphism of
X
in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
is
\delta^{P}_{X}
and

\pazocal{L}(F,b)(\delta^{P}_{X})=b_{X\times X}(\delta^{P}_{X})=\delta^{R}_{F(X)}=\id^{\mathscr{A}_{R}}_{F(X)}

thus
\pazocal{L}(F,b)
preserves identities. Let now
r\in P(X\times Y)
and
s\in P(Y\times Z)
. Then

\displaystyle\pazocal{L}(F,b)(s\underset{\mathscr{A}_{P}}{\circ}r)

\displaystyle=\pazocal{L}(F,b)\Bigl(\exists^{P}_{XYZ\to XZ}\bigl(P_{XYZ\to XY}(r)\wedge P_{XYZ\to YZ}(s)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=b_{X\times Z}\Bigl(\exists^{P}_{XYZ\to XZ}\bigl(P_{XYZ\to XY}(r)\wedge P_{XYZ\to YZ}(s)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists^{R}_{F(XYZ)\to F(XZ)}\Bigl(b_{X\times Y\times Z}\bigl(P_{XYZ\to XY}(r)\wedge P_{XYZ\to YZ}(s)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists^{R}_{F(XYZ)\to F(XZ)}\Bigl(b_{X\times Y\times Z}\bigl(P_{XYZ\to XY}(r)\bigr)\wedge b_{X\times Y\times Z}\bigl(P_{XYZ\to YZ}(s)\bigr)\!\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists^{R}_{F(XYZ)\to F(XZ)}\Bigl(R_{F(XYZ\to XY)}(b_{X\times Y}(r)\bigr)\wedge R_{F(XYZ\to YZ)}\bigl(b_{Y\times Z}(s)\bigr)\!\Bigr)

\displaystyle=b_{X\times Y}(s)\underset{\mathscr{A}_{R}}{\circ}b_{X\times Y}(r)

\displaystyle=\pazocal{L}(F,b)(s)\underset{\mathscr{A}_{R}}{\circ}\pazocal{L}(F,b)(r)

where we used the fact that
b
preserves existential quantifiers (third equation) and infima (fourth equation) and that
b
is natural (fifth equation). Therefore
\pazocal{L}(F,b)
is a functor, we need to check that it preserves all the structure of cartesian bicategory of
\mathscr{A}_{P}
.

Preservation of the monoidal product of
\mathscr{A}_{P}
is done in an analogous way to the proof above of preservation of compositions, where commutativity of
b
with existential quantifiers is not needed. Regarding the symmetry, recall that
\sigma^{\mathscr{A}_{P}}_{X,Y}=\Gamma_{P}(\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{X,Y})
, hence:

\displaystyle\pazocal{L}(F,b)(\sigma^{\mathscr{A}_{P}}_{X,Y})

\displaystyle=b_{X\times Y\times Y\times X}(\sigma^{\mathscr{A}_{P}}_{X,Y})

\displaystyle=b_{X\times Y\times Y\times X}\bigl(P_{\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{X,Y}\times\id_{Y\times X}}(\delta^{P}_{Y\times X})\bigr)

\displaystyle=R_{F(\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{X,Y}\times\id_{Y\times X})}\bigl(b_{Y\times X\times Y\times X}(\delta^{P}_{Y\times X})\bigr)

by naturality of
b
. Since
F
preserves the cartesian structure of
\mathbb{C}
, we have that
F(\sigma^{\mathbb{C}}_{X,Y}\times\id_{Y\times X})=\sigma^{\mathbb{D}}_{FX,FY}\times\id_{FY\times FX}
; moreover,
b_{Y\times X\times Y\times X}(\delta^{P}_{Y\times X})=\delta^{R}_{F(Y\times X)}
, therefore

\pazocal{L}(F,b)(\sigma^{\mathscr{A}_{P}}_{X,Y})=R_{\sigma^{\mathbb{D}}_{FX,FY}\times\id_{FY\times FX}}\bigl(\delta^{R}_{F(Y\times X)}\bigr)=\sigma^{\mathscr{A}_{R}}_{FX,FY}.

Analogously one shows that
\pazocal{L}(F,b)
preserves the comonoid structure of any object
X
in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
, since it is defined as the image along
\Gamma_{P}
of copying and discarding in
\mathbb{C}
.

Finally, monotonicity of
b
immediately implies that
\pazocal{L}(F,b)
preserves the partial order of
P(X\times Y)
for every
X,Y
in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
. This proves that
\pazocal{L}(F,b)
is indeed a morphism of cartesian bicategories.

It is left to prove that
\pazocal{L}
is functorial. It is immediate to see that for
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
,
\pazocal{L}(\id_{\mathbb{C}},\id_{P})=\id_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}
. As per preservation of compositions in
\mathsf{EED}
: let
R\colon\mathbb{D}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
and
S\colon\mathbb{E}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be elementary existential doctrines, and let
(F,b)\colon P\to R
and
(G,c)\colon R\to S
in
\mathsf{EED}
. We have that
(G,c)\circ(F,b)=(GF,cF\circ b)
, therefore

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GFX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}\bigl((G,c)\circ(F,b)\bigr)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times Y)\ni r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{F(X\times Y)}\bigl(b_{X\times Y}(r)\bigr)}

while

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GFX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FY}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GFY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}(F,b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}(G,c)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times Y)\ni r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{X\times Y}(r)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{FX\times FY}\bigl(b_{X\times Y}(r)\bigr)}

The two functors indeed coincide.
∎

Appendix C
Appendix to §
7

We first show that
\eta
(
10
) is a natural transformation in Lemma
47
and that
\varepsilon
(
9
) is a natural isomorphism in Lemma
48
, and then that they satisfy the triangular equalities required by the definition of adjunction in Theorem
49
.

Proof of Theorem
28

Lemma 47
.

There is a natural transformation

\eta\colon\id_{\mathsf{EED}}\to\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}

whose
P
-th component, for
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
an elementary existential doctrine, is
\eta_{P}=(\Gamma_{P},P\rho)
, where
(P\rho)_{X}=P_{\rho_{X}}
, with
\rho_{X}\colon X\times I\to X
the right unitor in
\mathbb{C}
(and
I
the terminal object).

Proof.

Let us fix
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
an elementary existential doctrine. Then
\pazocal{L}(P)=\mathscr{A}_{P}
, whose objects are the objects of
\mathbb{C}
, and hom-sets are
\mathscr{A}_{P}(X,Y)=P(X\times Y)
. This means that

\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)=\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(-,I)=P({-}\times I)\colon\Map({\mathscr{A}_{P}})^{\opposite}\to\InfSL.

To give a morphism
\eta_{P}\colon P\to\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)
in
\mathsf{EED}
means therefore to give a functor
F\colon\mathbb{C}\to\Map(\mathscr{A}_{P})
and a natural transformation
b\colon P\to P(F(-)\times I)
satisfying certain conditions. We have proved in Lemma
40
, Remark
41
and Lemma
42
that
\Gamma_{P}
is a functor whose image, in fact, is included in
\Map(\mathscr{A}_{P})
and, moreover, it is cartesian. Being the identity on objects, the natural transformation part of the definition of
\eta_{P}
must have components
b_{X}\colon P(X)\to P(X\times I)
, therefore
P\rho
type-checks. That
P\rho
is natural is obvious, being the whiskering of a functor with a natural transformation; we need to check that it preserves the equalities and existential quantifiers of
P
.

Regarding equalities, we have to show that
P_{\rho_{A\times A}}(\delta^{P}_{A})=\delta^{\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)}_{A}
. We have that

\delta^{\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)}_{A}=\scalebox{0.7}{\hbox to46.38pt{\vbox to24.48pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 21.83957pt\lower-12.23758pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{

{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{{}}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{

}

which is the composite, in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
, of
\Gamma_{P}{(\Delta_{A})}^{\opposite}
and
\Gamma_{P}(!\colon A\to I)
. Since
\Gamma_{P}(!\colon A\to I)=\top_{P(A\times I)}
, we have:

\delta^{\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)}_{A}=\exists_{A^{2}\times A\times I\to A^{2}\times I}\Bigl(P_{A^{2}\times A\times I\to A^{2}\times A}\bigl(P_{\id_{A^{2}}\times\Delta_{A}}(\delta^{P}_{A\times A})\bigr)\Bigr).

Using the Beck-Chevalley condition of
P
on the following pullback:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{2}\times A\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{2}\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{2}\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{3}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{A^{2}\times A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{A^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

and recalling that
\delta^{P}_{A\times A}=P_{\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{3}\rangle}(\delta^{P}_{A})\wedge P_{\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{4}\rangle}(\delta^{P}_{A})
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\delta^{\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)}_{A}

\displaystyle=P_{\rho_{A^{2}}}\Bigl(\exists_{A^{2}\times A\to A}\bigl(P_{\id_{A^{2}}\times\Delta_{A}}(P_{\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{3}\rangle}(\delta^{P}_{A})\wedge P_{\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{4}\rangle}(\delta^{P}_{A}))\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{\rho_{A^{2}}}\Bigl(\exists_{A^{2}\times A\to A^{2}}\bigl(P_{\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{3}\rangle\colon A^{3}\to A^{2}}(\delta^{P}_{A})\wedge P_{\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{3}\rangle\colon A^{3}\to A^{2}}(\delta^{P}_{A})\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{\rho_{A^{2}}}\Bigl(\exists_{A^{2}\times A\to A^{2}}\bigl(\exists_{\id_{A}\times\Delta_{A}}(\delta^{P}_{A})\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{\rho_{A^{2}}}(\delta^{P}_{A}).

Regarding the preservation of the existential quantifiers of

, we need to check that the square (

) commutes, which means that, for

})^{\opposite}=\top_{P(I\times X)}
. Therefore, if we annotate with subscripts different copies of the same object
A
to indicate the various projections in a concise way:

\displaystyle f\underset{\mathscr{A}_{P}}{\circ}{\lambda_{A}^{-1}}=\exists^{P}_{A_{1}IA_{2}XA_{3}\to A_{1}A_{3}}\Bigl(

\displaystyle P_{A_{1}IA_{2}XA_{3}\to A_{1}IA_{2}}\bigl(P_{\lambda_{A}^{-1}\times\id_{I\times A}}(\delta_{I\times A})\bigr)

\displaystyle\wedge P_{A_{1}IA_{2}XA_{3}\to A_{2}A_{3}}(\delta_{A})\Bigr).

Since, moreover,

\delta_{I\times A}=P_{IAIA\to II}(\delta_{I})\wedge P_{IAIA\to AA}(\delta_{A})=\top\wedge P_{IAIA\to AA}(\delta_{A})=P_{IAIA\to AA}(\delta_{A}),

we have

\displaystyle f\underset{\mathscr{A}_{P}}{\circ}{\lambda_{A}^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\exists_{A_{1}IA_{2}XA_{3}\to A_{1}XA_{3}}\bigl(P_{A_{1}IA_{2}XA_{3}\to A_{1}A_{2}}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{A_{1}IA_{2}XA_{3}\to A_{2}A_{3}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{A_{1}IA_{2}XA_{3}\to A_{1}XA_{3}}\Bigl(P_{A_{1}IA_{2}XA_{3}\to A_{1}IA_{2}X}\bigl(P_{A_{1}IA_{2}X\to A_{1}A_{2}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 100.00015pt\wedge P_{A_{1}IA_{2}XA_{3}\to A_{2}A_{3}}(\delta_{A})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{A_{1}IA_{2}XA_{3}\to A_{1}XA_{3}}\Bigl(\exists_{A_{1}IA_{2}X\to A_{1}IA_{2}XA_{2}}\bigl(P_{A_{1}IA_{2}X\to A_{1}A_{2}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{A_{1}IA_{2}X\to A_{1}XA_{2}}\bigl(P_{A_{1}IA_{2}X\to A_{1}A_{2}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{A_{1}XA_{2}\to A_{1}IA_{2}X}\bigl(P_{A_{1}IA_{2}X\to A_{1}A_{2}}(\delta_{A})\bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{A_{1}XA_{2}\to A_{1}A_{2}}(\delta_{A}).

Composing the above with
P_{\rho_{X\times A}}(r)
, we obtain the right-hand side of (
13
), as follows:

\displaystyle\exists^{\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)}_{\Gamma(\pi)}\bigl(P_{\rho_{X\times A}}(r)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{AXAI\to AI}\Bigl(P_{AXAI\to AXA}\bigl(P_{AXA\to AA}(\delta_{A})\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 65.00009pt\wedge P_{A_{1}XA_{2}I\to XA_{2}I}\bigl(P_{\rho_{X\times A}}(r)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{AXAI\to AI}\bigl(P_{AXAI\to AA}(\delta_{A})\wedge P_{A_{1}XA_{2}I\to XA_{2}}(r)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{AXAI\to AI}\Bigl(P_{A_{1}XA_{2}I\to XA_{2}I}\bigl(P_{XA_{2}I\to XA_{2}}(r)\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 65.00009pt\wedge P_{AXAI\to AA}(\delta_{A})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{AXAI\to AI}\Bigl(\exists_{XAI\to AXAI}\bigl(P_{XAI\to XA}(r)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\exists_{XAI\to AI}\bigl(P_{\rho_{X\times A}}(r)\bigr).

Therefore equation (
13
) reduces to

P_{\rho_{A}}\bigl(\exists^{P}_{\pi}(r)\bigr)=\exists^{P}_{XAI\to AI}\bigl(P_{\rho_{X\times A}}(r)\bigr)

which holds because the following square is a pullback in
\mathbb{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times A\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{3}\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{X\times A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

therefore we can use the Beck-Chevalley condition of
P
.

Finally, we prove that
\eta
is a natural transformation. Let
R\colon\mathbb{D}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
and
(F,c)\colon P\to R
be a morphism in
\mathsf{EED}
. Then

\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(F,c)=\bigl(\pazocal{L}(F,c)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\Map(\mathscr{A}_{P})},b^{\pazocal{L}(F,c)}\bigr)

where recall that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FY}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}(F,c)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times Y)\ni r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{X\times Y}(r)}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(FX\times I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{X\times I}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{\pazocal{L}(F,c)}}

We then need to prove that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(R)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F,c)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(F,c)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{R}}

(14)

commutes in
\mathsf{EED}
. In the first component, we are asking for the following square of functors to commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(\mathscr{A}_{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(\mathscr{A}_{R})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}(F,c)\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\Map(\mathscr{A}_{P})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{R}}

The upper and lower legs send a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{C}
to

c_{X\times Y}\bigl(P_{f\times\id_{Y}}(\delta^{P}_{Y})\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad R_{Ff\times\id_{FY}}(\delta^{R}_{FY})

respectively, and those expressions are the same map in
\mathscr{A}_{R}
due to the naturality condition of
c\colon P\to RF^{\opposite}
applied to the morphism
f\times\id_{Y}\colon X\times Y\to Y\times Y
, which states that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RF(X\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(Y\times Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RF(Y\times Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{X\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{f\times\id_{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{Y\times Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RF_{f\times\id_{Y}}=R_{Ff\times\id_{FY}}}

commutes, hence, given that
\delta^{P}_{Y}\in P(Y\times Y)
, we have

c_{X\times Y}\bigl(P_{f\times\id_{Y}}(\delta^{P}_{Y})\bigr)=R_{Ff\times\id_{FY}}\bigl(c_{Y\times Y}(\delta_{Y})\bigr)=R_{Ff\times\id_{FY}}(\delta^{R}_{FY}).

In the second component, (
14
) becomes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(-\times I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(-\times I)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{-\times I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\rho}

which, on the
X
-th component, is a square in
\InfSL
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RF(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(FX\times I)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\rho_{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{X\times I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\rho_{FX})}

(Notice that
\rho_{FX}=F(\rho_{X})
because
F
is strict cartesian.) This square does indeed commute due to the naturality of
c
applied to the morphism
\rho_{X}\colon X\to X\times I
in
\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}
.
∎

It is convenient to analyse the category
\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})
for a cartesian bicategory
\mathbb{B}
. Recall that
\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})=\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(-,I)
and that, for
f\colon X\to Y
in
\Map(\mathbb{B})
and
u\colon Y\to I
morphism in
\mathbb{B}
,
\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})_{f}(u)=u\circ f\colon X\to I
. By definition then,
\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})=\mathscr{A}_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
has the same objects of
\mathbb{B}
while a morphism
X\to Y
in
\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})
is an element of
\mathbb{B}(X\times Y,I)
.

•

Composition.

If
R\colon X\times Y\to I
and
S\colon Y\times Z\to I
are morphisms of
\mathbb{B}
, then

\displaystyle S\underset{\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}{\circ}R

\displaystyle=\exists_{XYZ\to XZ}^{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}\bigl(\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})_{XYZ\to XY}(R)\wedge\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})_{XYZ\to YZ}(S)\bigr)

(where the above projections appearing in the subscripts are projections in
\Map(\mathbb{B})
, which are given by

and the unitors).

•

Monoidal product.
Take
R\colon X\times X^{\prime}\to I
and
S\colon Y\times Y^{\prime}\to I
in
\mathbb{B}
, which means that
R\colon X\to X^{\prime}
and
S\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}
in
\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})
. Then

\displaystyle R\otimes S

\displaystyle=\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})_{XYX^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\to XX^{\prime}}(R)\land\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})_{XYX^{\prime}Y^{\prime}\to YY^{\prime}}(S)

(16)

Lemma 48
.

There is a natural isomorphism

\varepsilon\colon\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}\to\id_{\mathsf{CBC}}

whose
\mathbb{B}
-th component, for
\mathbb{B}
a cartesian bicategory, sends
R\in\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})(X,Y)=\mathbb{B}(X\times Y,I)
to the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{X}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{Y}}

where
\rho
and
\lambda
are, respectively, the right and left unitors in
\mathbb{B}
and
\bar{R}
is:

\scriptstyle{Y}
R
\scriptstyle X

(17)

The inverse
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}^{-1}
of
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}
sends
S\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{B}
to

\scriptstyle S
\scriptstyle X
\scriptstyle Y
\scriptstyle Y

Proof.

The plan of the proof is as follows: first we show that
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}^{-1}
are inverse of each other as functions on hom-sets, in the sense that for any
R\colon X\times Y\to I
and
S\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{B}
,
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}(R)=R
and
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}(S)=S
. Next we observe that
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}
and
\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(B)}
coincide on
\Map(\mathbb{B})
. This immediately implies that
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}
preserves symmetries and the comonoid structure on every object of
\mathbb{B}
, while the Snake Lemma will imply that the same holds for
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}
. Then we prove that both
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}
and its inverse are strict monoidal functors, thus morphisms in
\mathsf{CBC}
. Finally, we show naturality of
\varepsilon
.

First, from the snake equations (
5
) it immediately follows that for any
R\colon X\times Y\to I
and
S\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{B}
,
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}(R)=R
and
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}(S)=S
, so
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}^{-1}
are inverse of each other as functions on hom-sets.

Next, notice that

{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}(S)=\delta^{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}_{Y}\circ(S\times\id_{Y})=\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}(S)

the last equation holding if and only if
S
is a map in
\mathbb{B}
(otherwise we cannot compute
\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
on it). This means that
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}
is an extension of
\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
to all of
\mathbb{B}
. Since the symmetry in
\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})
is defined as
\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}(\sigma^{\Map(\mathbb{B})})=\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}(\sigma^{\mathbb{B}})
and
\sigma^{\mathbb{B}}
is a map in
\mathbb{B}
, we have

{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}(\sigma^{\mathbb{B}})=\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}(\sigma^{\mathbb{B}})=\sigma^{\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}.

The same can be said for the comultiplication and the counit of every object
X
in
\mathbb{B}
. Applying
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}
to both sides of the above equation, one can see that the same holds for
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}
, therefore
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}
preserve symmetries and the comonoid structure of every object of
\mathbb{B}
and
\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})
respectively. Preservation of the ordering of the hom-sets is guaranteed by the fact that tensor and composition in
\mathbb{B}
are monotone operations.

We proceed by showing that
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}
and
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}
are strict monoidal functors.

Given
R\colon X\times Y\to I
and
S\colon Y\times Z\to I
in
\mathbb{B}
, from (
15
) we get that

\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}(s\circ r)=\scalebox{0.7}{\hbox to82.63pt{\vbox to77.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 45.87914pt\lower-36.06595pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{

}\quad=\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}(s)\circ\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}(r).

Preservation of the identity is a direct consequence of the snake equations (
5
). Therefore
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}
is a functor: we need to show that it is strict monoidal. Take now
R\colon X\otimes X^{\prime}\to I
and
S\colon Y\otimes Y^{\prime}\to I
: by (
15
), we have

}}={\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}(R)\otimes{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}(S).

This proves that
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}
and
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{\mathbb{B}}
are inverse morphisms in
\mathsf{CBC}
.

All that is left to do is to prove the naturality of
\varepsilon
. To that end, let
F\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
be a morphism in
\mathsf{CBC}
: we show that the following square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{\mathbb{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}}

commutes. We have that
\pazocal{R}(F)=({F}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\Map(\mathbb{A})},b^{F})
where for
u\colon A\to I
in
\mathbb{A}
,
b^{F}_{A}(u)=F(u)
. Therefore
\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(F)\colon\mathscr{A}_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{A})}\to\mathscr{A}_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
is the functor that maps
R\in\mathbb{A}(X\times Y,I)
into
F(R)\in\mathbb{B}(FX\times FY,I)
. Hence the square above acts on
R\colon X\times Y\to I
in
\mathbb{A}
as follows:

R
\mapsto
R
F(R)
F(R)
\mapsto

\mapsto

\mapsto

FX
FY
X
X
Y
Y
FX
FY

where the right vertical arrow is correct because
F
preserves all the structure of cartesian bicategory. Thus
\varepsilon
is natural.
∎

Theorem 49
.

\pazocal{L}
is left adjoint to
\pazocal{R}
.

Proof.

All that is left to do is to show that the triangular equalities (
8
) are satisfied. The first one requires that, for
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
an elementary existential doctrine, the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}(P)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}(\eta_{P})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\pazocal{L}(P)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{\pazocal{L}(P)}}

(18)

Recall that
\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)=\mathscr{A}_{\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)}
, therefore its objects are those of the domain category of the doctrine

\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)=\pazocal{R}(\mathscr{A}_{P})=\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(-,I)\colon\Map(\mathscr{A}_{P})^{\opposite}\to\InfSL

while its homsets are

\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)(X,Y)=\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)(X\times Y)=\Hom_{\mathscr{A}_{P}}(X\times Y,I)=P(X\times Y\times I).

By definition of
\pazocal{L}
and
\varepsilon
, we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{L}(\eta_{P})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times Y)\ni R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\rho_{X\times Y}}(R)\in P(X\times Y\times I)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{\pazocal{L}(P)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(X\times Y\times I)\ni Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{P}({\rho}^{-1}_{X})\mathrel{;}\bar{Q}\mathrel{;}\Gamma(\lambda_{Y})}

where
\bar{Q}
is given as in (
17
). Therefore
\varepsilon_{\pazocal{L}(P)}\circ\pazocal{L}(\eta_{P})
is the identity on objects and its action on
R\in P(X\times Y)
is

\Gamma_{P}\left({\scalebox{0.7}{\hbox to46.38pt{\vbox to43.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 24.53957pt\lower-21.83957pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{

(19)

where the above composite is performed in

.

In the following calculations, we will have to refer to various instances of the two objects
X
and
Y
, and we will do so as annotated in the following string diagram in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
, showing
\varepsilon_{\pazocal{L}(P)}\circ\pazocal{L}(\eta_{P})(R)
:

Y_{1}
X_{1}
P_{\rho_{X\times Y}}(R)
X_{2}
Y_{2}
Y_{3}
Y_{4}

In this sense, the first morphism in the chain (
19
) goes from
X_{1}
to
X_{2}\otimes Y_{1}\otimes Y_{2}
(as a morphism in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
), the middle one goes from
X_{2}\otimes Y_{1}\otimes Y_{2}
to
I\otimes Y_{3}
, whereas
\Gamma_{P}(\lambda_{Y})
goes from
I\otimes Y_{3}
to
Y_{4}
. (Again, the use of subscripts is only a notational convenience to clarify which projections we are taking into consideration in the following computations; as objects of
\mathbb{C}
, we have
X=X_{1}=X_{2}
and
Y=Y_{1}=Y_{2}=Y_{3}=Y_{4}
.)

We begin by computing the middle morphism:

\displaystyle P_{\rho_{X\times Y}}(R)\otimes\delta_{Y}

\displaystyle=P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to X_{2}Y_{1}I}\bigl(P_{\rho_{X\times Y}}(R)\bigr)\land P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to Y_{2}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y})

\displaystyle=P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to X_{2}Y_{1}}(R)\land P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to Y_{2}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y})

therefore we have that
(P_{\rho_{X\times Y}}(R)\otimes\delta_{Y})\mathrel{;}\Gamma_{P}(\lambda_{Y})
is equal to:

\displaystyle\exists_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}\Bigl[P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}}\bigl(P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to X_{2}Y_{1}}(R)

\displaystyle\hskip 210.00032pt\land P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to Y_{2}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y})\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 110.00017pt\land P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{4}\to IY_{3}Y_{4}}\bigl(P_{\lambda_{Y}\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y})\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exists_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}\Bigl[P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}}(R)

\displaystyle\hskip 120.00018pt\land P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{4}\to Y_{2}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y})\land P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{4}\to Y_{3}Y_{4}}(\delta_{Y})\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exists_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}\Bigl[P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}}(R)

\displaystyle\hskip 120.00018pt\land\exists_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{3}}\bigl(P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to Y_{2}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y})\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exists_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}\Bigl[P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}\bigl(P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}}(R)\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 120.00018pt\land\exists_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{3}}\bigl(P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to Y_{2}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y})\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}}(R)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\land\exists_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}\Bigl[\exists_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}Y_{3}}\bigl(P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to Y_{2}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y})\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}}(R)\land\exists_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}}\bigl(P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to Y_{2}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y})\bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}}(R)\land P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}\to Y_{2}Y_{3}}(\delta_{Y}).

The last equation above is due to the fact that the morphism
e=X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}IY_{3}\to X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}
is an isomorphism and therefore the left adjoint of
P_{e}
, which is
\exists_{e}
, is actually
P_{{e}^{-1}}
. Hence, the composite (
19
) is equal to:

\displaystyle\exists_{X_{1}X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{1}Y_{4}}\Bigl[P_{X_{1}X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{1}X_{2}}(\delta_{X})\land P_{X_{1}X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{2}Y_{1}}(R)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.00012pt\land P_{X_{1}X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to Y_{1}Y_{2}}(\delta_{Y})\land P_{X_{1}X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to Y_{2}Y_{4}}(\delta_{Y})\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exists_{X_{1}X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{1}Y_{4}}\Bigl[\exists_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to XXY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}\bigl(P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to XY_{1}}(R)\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 100.00015pt\land P_{X_{1}X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to Y_{1}Y_{2}}(\delta_{Y})\land P_{X_{1}X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to Y_{2}Y_{4}}(\delta_{Y})\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exists_{X_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{1}Y_{4}}

\displaystyle\hskip 13.50008pt\exists_{X_{1}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}

\displaystyle\hskip 13.50008pt\exists_{X_{1}X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{1}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}\Bigl[P_{X_{1}X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{1}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}\bigl(P_{X_{1}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to Y_{1}Y_{2}}(\delta_{Y})

\displaystyle\hskip 220.00034pt\land P_{X_{1}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to Y_{2}Y_{4}}(\delta_{Y})\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 120.00018pt\land\exists_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to XXY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}\bigl(P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to XY_{1}}(R)\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exists_{X_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{1}Y_{4}}

\displaystyle\hskip 13.50008pt\exists_{X_{1}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}\Bigl[P_{X_{1}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to Y_{1}Y_{2}}(\delta_{Y})\land P_{X_{1}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to Y_{2}Y_{4}}(\delta_{Y})

\displaystyle\hskip 100.00015pt\land\exists_{X_{1}X_{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{1}Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}

\displaystyle\hskip 111.00023pt\exists_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to XXY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}}\bigl(P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to XY_{1}}(R)\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exists_{XY_{2}Y_{4}\to XY_{4}}

\displaystyle\hskip 13.50008pt\exists_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to XY_{2}Y_{4}}\Bigl[P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to XY_{2}Y_{4}}\bigl(P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to Y_{2}Y_{4}}(\delta_{Y})\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 90.00014pt\land P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to Y_{1}Y_{2}}(\delta_{Y})\land P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to XY_{1}}(R)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exists_{XY_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{1}Y_{4}}\Bigl[P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to Y_{2}Y_{4}}(\delta_{Y})

\displaystyle\hskip 70.0001pt\land\exists_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to XY_{2}Y_{4}}\exists_{XY_{1}Y_{4}\to XY_{1}Y_{1}Y_{4}}\bigl(P_{XY_{1}Y_{4}\to XY_{1}}(R)\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exists_{XY_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{1}Y_{4}}\Bigl[P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}Y_{4}\to Y_{2}Y_{4}}(\delta_{Y})\land P_{XY_{1}Y_{4}\to XY_{1}}(R)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\exists_{XY_{2}Y_{4}\to X_{1}Y_{4}}\exists_{XY\to XYY}(R)

\displaystyle=R.

This proves that triangle (
18
) commutes.

The second triangular equality requires that, for
\mathbb{B}
a cartesian bicategory, the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pazocal{R}(\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}})}

(20)

Recall that
\eta_{\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}=(\Gamma_{\pazocal{R}(B)},\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})\rho)
, where

coincides with the restriction of
{\varepsilon}^{-1}_{B}
(see (

as shown in the proof of Lemma

, while

(\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})\rho)_{X}=\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(-,I)(\rho_{X})=-\circ\rho_{X}\colon\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(X,I)\to\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(X\otimes I,I)

as

is the right unitor of
\mathbb{B}
.

Now,

\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})=\Hom_{\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B})}(-,I)=\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(-\otimes I,I)\colon\Map(\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B}))\to\InfSL

and

\pazocal{R}(\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}})=(\varepsilon_{B}\mathord{\upharpoonright}_{\Map(\pazocal{L}\pazocal{R}(\mathbb{B}))},b^{\varepsilon_{\mathbb{B}}})

where by (

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(X\otimes I,I)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}

and it it easy to see that, for

Composition in

we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(\mathbb{B})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{B}}(X,I)}

Appendix D
Appendix to §
8

We first characterise the maps of
\mathscr{A}_{P}
in Proposition
50
, which we need to prove that the Rule of Unique Choice is equivalent to the fullness of the graph functor in Proposition
51
. The fact that a doctrine
P
has comprehensive diagonals if and only if
\Gamma_{P}
is faithful is stated in
[
24
, Theorem 3.2]
. Finally, we give a proof of Theorem
35
.

Proposition 50

(Cf.
[
24
]
)
.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
be an elementary existential doctrine,
R\in P(X\times Y)
. Then
R
is a map in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
if and only if

\displaystyle\exists_{\pi_{1}\colon XY\to X}(R)=\top_{PX}

(21)

\displaystyle P_{\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle\colon XYY\to XY}(R)\land P_{\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{3}\rangle\colon XYY\to XY}(R)\leq P_{\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{3}\rangle\colon XYY\to YY}(\delta_{Y}).

(22)

Proof.

Equation (
21
) stems from calculating the two sides of

=
R
\scriptstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{X}

while (
22
) is equivalent to

P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}\to XY_{1}}(R)\land P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}\to Y_{1}Y_{2}}(\delta_{Y})=P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}\to XY_{1}}(R)\land P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}\to XY_{2}}(R)

which is the result of computing the two sides of the equation

R
\scriptstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{X}
R
R
=
\scriptstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{Y}
\scriptstyle{X}
\scriptstyle{Y}

in the category
\mathscr{A}_{P}
.
∎

Proposition 51
.

An elementary existential doctrine
P
satisfies the Rule of Unique Choice if and only if its graph functor
\Gamma_{P}
is full.

Proof.

We show that for
P\colon\mathbb{C}^{\opposite}\to\InfSL
,
R\in P(X\times Y)
map in
\mathscr{A}_{P}
and
h\colon X\to Y
in
\mathbb{C}
:

\top_{PX}\leq P_{\langle\id_{X},h\rangle}(R)\iff R=P_{h\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y}).

First, if
R=P_{h\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y})
, then

P_{\langle\id_{X},h\rangle}(R)=P_{\langle\id_{X},h\rangle}P_{h\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y})=P_{\langle h,h\rangle}(\delta_{Y})=P_{h}P_{\Delta_{Y}}(\delta_{Y})=P_{h}(\top_{PY})=\top_{PX}

because
\delta_{Y}=\exists_{\Delta_{Y}}(\top_{PY})
and, since
\exists_{\Delta}
is left adjoint to
P_{\Delta}
,
\top_{PY}\leq P_{\Delta_{Y}}\exists_{\Delta_{Y}}(\top_{PY})
.
Vice versa, suppose that
\top_{PX}\leq P_{\langle\id_{X},h\rangle}(R)
. Then
P_{XY\to X}(\top_{PX})=P_{XY\to X}P_{\langle\id_{X},h\rangle}(R)
. Now, since the following two diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\id_{X},h\rangle\times\id_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\id_{X},h\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\times B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times Y\times Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\times\id_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\id_{X},h\rangle\times\id_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\pi_{2},\pi_{3}\rangle}

we have:

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=R\land\top_{P(XY)}=R\land P_{XY\to X}P_{\langle\id_{X},h\rangle}(R)

\displaystyle=P_{\langle\id_{X},h\rangle\times\id_{Y}}\bigl(P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}\to XY_{2}}(R)\land P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}\to XY_{1}}(R)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq P_{\langle\id_{X},h\rangle\times\id_{Y}}\bigl(P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}\to Y_{1}Y_{2}}(\delta_{Y})\bigr)=P_{h\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y})

where the inequality above is due to (
22
). So we proved that
R\leq P_{h\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y})
, while for the other inequality:

\displaystyle P_{h\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y})

\displaystyle=P_{h\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y})\land\top_{P(XY)}

\displaystyle=P_{h\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y})\land P_{XY\to X}P_{\langle\id_{X},h\rangle}(R)

\displaystyle=P_{\langle\id_{X},h\rangle\times\id_{Y}}\bigl(\exists_{\id_{X}\times\Delta_{Y}}(R)\bigr)

because of (
1
). Since
R=P_{\id_{X}\times\Delta_{Y}}P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}\to XY_{2}}(R)
, by adjunction we have
\exists_{\id_{X}\times\Delta_{Y}}(R)\leq P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}\to XY_{2}}(R)
, hence we get

P_{h\times\id_{Y}}(\delta_{Y})\leq P_{\langle\id_{X},h\rangle\times\id_{Y}}P_{XY_{1}Y_{2}\to XY_{2}}(R)=R.\qed

Proof of Theorem
35

By restricting
\pazocal{L}
and corestricting
\pazocal{R}
to
\overline{\mathsf{EED}}
, one obtains two functors
\overline{\pazocal{L}}
and
\overline{\pazocal{R}}
and we can define two natural transformations
\overline{\eta}
and
\overline{\varepsilon}
whose individual components are the same as
\eta
and
\varepsilon
respectively. This means that
\overline{\eta}
and
\overline{\varepsilon}
are still natural transformations satisfying the triangular equalities (
8
), therefore
\overline{\pazocal{L}}\dashv\overline{\pazocal{R}}
, with
\overline{\varepsilon}
being a natural isomorphism.

Let
P\colon\mathbb{C}\to\InfSL
be in
\overline{\mathsf{EED}}
. Then
\Gamma_{P}
is faithful because of
[
24
, Theorem 3.2]
and full because of Proposition
51
. Hence
\overline{\eta}_{P}=(\Gamma_{P},P\rho)
has an inverse in
\overline{\mathsf{EED}}
, namely
({\Gamma}^{-1}_{P},P{\rho}^{-1})
, where
{\Gamma}^{-1}_{P}
is the identity on objects and sends every map
R\colon X\to Y
in
\pazocal{R}\pazocal{L}(P)
into the unique morphism
f\colon X\to Y
of
\mathbb{C}
such that
\Gamma_{P}(f)=R
. Therefore also
\overline{\eta}
is a natural isomorphism, hence
(\overline{\pazocal{L}},\overline{\pazocal{R}},\overline{\eta},\overline{\varepsilon})
is an adjoint equivalence. ∎
